In fact, these first moment estimates are stronger by those derived directly from the second moment estimates, indicating the essential role of the order of taking expectation and absolute value in this setting.

An important feature of the bounds in Proposition
8.2
above is that when
\Sigma_{XY}=0
, all estimates reduce to
0
. Furthermore the exponent in
\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F},\tau_{X},\tau_{Y}
also need be correct to allow precise mean and variance expansions in Theorems
8.4
and
9.12
, and therefore the non-null CLT in Theorem
2.2
, under the entire high dimensional regime
n\wedge p\wedge q\to\infty
. It is for this reason that the proof of Proposition
8.2
is rather involved, the details of which can be found in Appendix
C
. The following lemma is representative in terms of an interpolation technique in proving Proposition
8.2
and may be of broader interest.

Lemma 8.3
.

Suppose the spectrum of
\Sigma
is contained in
[1/M,M]
for some
M>1
. Let
\mathfrak{h}_{X},\mathfrak{h}_{Y}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be smooth functions. For any
k,k^{\prime},\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\{1,2\}
, define

\displaystyle\psi_{\mathfrak{h}_{X},\mathfrak{h}_{Y}}(\Sigma_{XY})\equiv\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathfrak{h}_{X}\big(L_{X}(X_{k},X_{\ell})\big)\mathfrak{h}_{Y}\big(L_{Y}(Y_{k^{\prime}},Y_{\ell^{\prime}})\big)\big\lvert X_{1},Y_{1}\Big].

Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big(\psi_{\mathfrak{h}_{X},\mathfrak{h}_{Y}}(\Sigma_{XY})-\psi_{\mathfrak{h}_{X},\mathfrak{h}_{Y}}(0)\big)^{2}\lesssim\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}(1\vee\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2})

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigg(\tau_{X}^{-4}\cdot\mathbb{E}^{1/4}(\mathfrak{h}_{X}^{\prime}\circ L_{X})^{8}\cdot\mathbb{E}^{1/4}(\mathfrak{h}_{Y}\circ L_{Y})^{8}+\tau_{Y}^{-4}\cdot\mathbb{E}^{1/4}(\mathfrak{h}_{Y}^{\prime}\circ L_{Y})^{8}\cdot\mathbb{E}^{1/4}(\mathfrak{h}_{X}\circ L_{X})^{8}\bigg).

The constants in
\lesssim
only depend on
M
. The claims remain valid with
X=Y
when the spectrum of
\Sigma_{X}=\Sigma_{Y}
is contained in
[1/M,M]
for some
M>1
.

Lemma
8.3
gives a general recipe of bounding the second moment, in terms of the dependence measure
\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}
. Details see Appendix
C
.

8.2.
Mean expansion

As a quick application of the residual estimates in Proposition
8.2
, we may get the following mean expansion.

Theorem 8.4
.

Suppose that the spectrum of
\Sigma
is contained in
[1/M,M]
for some
M>1
. Then the following expansion holds for the distance covariance:

\displaystyle m_{\Sigma}=\mathbb{E}_{\Sigma}\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})=\dcov^{2}(X,Y)=\frac{\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}}{\tau_{X}\tau_{Y}}\Big[1+\mathcal{O}\big((\tau_{X}\wedge\tau_{Y})^{-1}\big)\Big].

The constants in
\mathcal{O}
only depend on
M
and the distribution of
Z_{1}
via its Poincaré constant
c_{\ast}
and
\varepsilon_{0}
prescribed in Assumption
A
. The claims remain valid with
X=Y
when the spectrum of
\Sigma_{X}=\Sigma_{Y}
is contained in
[1/M,M]
for some
M>1
.

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle\dcov^{2}(X,Y)=\mathbb{E}\big[U(X_{1},X_{2})V(Y_{1},Y_{2})\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tau_{X}\tau_{Y}}\Big[\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}-\tau_{X}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big(\bar{R}_{X}(X_{1},X_{2})Y_{1}^{\top}Y_{2}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad-\tau_{X}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big(\bar{R}_{Y}(Y_{1},Y_{2})X_{1}^{\top}X_{2}\big)+\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big(\bar{R}_{X}(X_{1},X_{2})\bar{R}_{Y}(Y_{1},Y_{2})\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{\tau_{X}\tau_{Y}}\bigg[\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}-\tau_{X}^{2}\mathbb{E}\psi_{X}(X_{1},X_{2})-\tau_{Y}^{2}\mathbb{E}\psi_{Y}(Y_{1},Y_{2})+\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}\mathbb{E}\psi_{X,Y}(X_{1},Y_{1})\bigg].

The claim now follows by invoking Proposition
8.2
-(1).
∎

A stochastic version of the above theorem was previously derived in
[
ZZYS20
, Theorem 2.1.1]
, where the main term
\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}/(\tau_{X}\tau_{Y})
was replaced by an unbiased estimator and the remainder term was controlled at the order
(p\wedge q)^{-1/2}
. In comparison, thanks to the sharp residual estimates in Proposition
8.2
, our bound for the remainder term is much more refined in that it contains an important multiplicative factor
\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}
, which makes it asymptotically negligible in the null case as well.

9.
Hoeffding decomposition and variance expansion

We first review the basics of Hoeffding decomposition that will be relevant to our purpose. Following
[
Ser80
, Section 5.1.5, pp. 177]
, for a generic 4-th order
U
-statistic with symmetric kernel
k:\mathcal{Z}^{4}\to\mathbb{R}
, let

\displaystyle k_{c}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{c})\equiv\mathbb{E}[k(z_{1},\ldots,z_{c},Z_{c+1},\ldots,Z_{4})],\quad z_{1},\ldots,z_{c}\in\mathcal{Z},

and for any
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4}\in\mathcal{Z}
,

\displaystyle g_{0}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathbb{E}k(\bm{Z}),\quad g_{1}(z_{1})\equiv k_{1}(z_{1})-\mathbb{E}k(\bm{Z}),

\displaystyle g_{2}(z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle\equiv k_{2}(z_{1},z_{2})-k_{1}(z_{1})-k_{2}(z_{2})+\mathbb{E}k(\bm{Z}),

\displaystyle g_{3}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})

\displaystyle\equiv k_{3}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})-\mathbb{E}k(\bm{Z})-\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}g_{1}(z_{\ell})-\sum_{1\leq\ell_{1}<\ell_{2}\leq 3}g_{2}\big(z_{\ell_{1}},z_{\ell_{2}}\big),

\displaystyle g_{4}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4})

\displaystyle\equiv k_{4}\big(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4}\big)-\mathbb{E}k(\bm{Z})-\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}g_{1}(z_{\ell})

(9.1)

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{1\leq\ell_{1}<\ell_{2}\leq 3}g_{2}\big(z_{\ell_{1}},z_{\ell_{2}}\big)-\sum_{1\leq\ell_{1}<\ell_{2}<\ell_{3}\leq 4}g_{3}\big(z_{\ell_{1}},z_{\ell_{2}},z_{\ell_{3}}\big).

Then the Hoeffding decomposition says that

\displaystyle U_{n}(k)=\sum_{c=0}^{4}\binom{4}{c}U_{n}(g_{c}).

Here for a generic symmetric kernel
g:\mathcal{Z}^{c}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle U_{n}(g)\equiv\begin{cases}\binom{n}{c}^{-1}\sum_{i_{1}<\ldots<i_{c}}g\big(z_{i_{1}},\ldots,z_{i_{c}}\big),&c\geq 1;\\
g&c=0.\end{cases}

For
c=0
,
g
is understood as a real number. In what follows, we will take
\mathcal{Z}\equiv\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
, and
k
as the kernel defined in Proposition
2.1
. We will evaluate the variance of
\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})=U_{n}(k)
by evaluating the variance of
g_{1},g_{2},g_{3},g_{4}
associated with
k
as defined above.

9.1.
Hoeffding decomposition: 1st order

The goal of this subsection is to prove the following variance expansion for the first-order kernel associated with
k
.

Proposition 9.1
.

Suppose the spectrum of
\Sigma
is contained in
[1/M,M]
for some
M>1
. Then for any
\varepsilon>0
, the first-order variance is given by

\displaystyle\binom{4}{1}^{2}\binom{n}{1}^{-1}\mathbb{E}g_{1}^{2}(X_{1},Y_{1})=(1\pm\varepsilon)\cdot\bar{\sigma}_{n,1}^{2}(X,Y)\cdot\bigg[1+\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{1}{\varepsilon\cdot(\tau_{X}\wedge\tau_{Y})}\Big)\bigg].

Here
\bar{\sigma}_{n,1}^{2}(X,Y)
is as defined in Theorem
2.2
, and the constants in
\mathcal{O}
only depend on
M
and the distribution of
Z_{1}
only via its Poincaré constant
c_{\ast}
, excess kurtosis
\kappa
, and
\varepsilon_{0}
prescribed in Assumption
A
.
The claim remains valid with
X=Y
when the spectrum of
\Sigma_{X}=\Sigma_{Y}
is contained in
[1/M,M]
for some
M>1
.

The proof of the above proposition will be presented towards the end of this subsection. First, we may compute:

Lemma 9.2
.

The first order kernel is given by

\displaystyle k_{1}(z_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}k(z_{1},Z_{2},Z_{3},Z_{4})=\frac{1}{2}\bigg[\mathbb{E}U(x_{1},X)V(y_{1},Y)+\dcov^{2}(X,Y)\bigg],

\displaystyle g_{1}(z_{1})

\displaystyle=k_{1}(z_{1})-\mathbb{E}k(Z)=\frac{1}{2}\bigg[\mathbb{E}U(x_{1},X)V(y_{1},Y)-\dcov^{2}(X,Y)\bigg].

We will use the above lemma to devise an expansion for
g_{1}
. From the approximation of
U,V
in (
6.2
), one may hope that the main term for
g_{1}
would be
2^{-1}\mathbb{E}U(x_{1},X)V(y_{1},Y)\approx(x_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}y_{1}-\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2})/2\tau_{X}\tau_{Y}
. As announced in Section
6
, this is however not the case. Let the ‘main term’ be defined as

(9.2)

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{1}(x_{1},y_{1})\equiv\frac{1}{2\tau_{X}\tau_{Y}}\bigg[\big(x_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}y_{1}-\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}\big)+\mathscr{A}_{1,X}(x_{1},y_{1})+\mathscr{A}_{1,Y}(x_{1},y_{1})\bigg],

where

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{1,X}(x_{1},y_{1})

\displaystyle\equiv-\frac{\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}}{2\tau_{X}^{2}}\big(\lVert x_{1}\rVert^{2}-\tr(\Sigma_{X})\big),\quad\,\mathscr{A}_{1,Y}(x_{1},y_{1})\equiv-\frac{\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}}{2\tau_{Y}^{2}}\big(\lVert y_{1}\rVert^{2}-\tr(\Sigma_{Y})\big).

The terms
\mathscr{A}_{1,X}(x_{1},y_{1}),\mathscr{A}_{1,Y}(x_{1},y_{1})
are essential to correct the naive approximation (
6.2
), in that these terms contribute to the somewhat difficult terms of negative sign in the variance expansion of
\bar{g}_{1}
in Lemma
9.4
, which cannot be neglected as they may have the same order as that of the leading terms.

With the main term defined above, let the ‘residual term’ be defined by

(9.3)

\displaystyle\bar{R}_{1}(x_{1},y_{1})\equiv-\tau_{X}^{2}\bar{\psi}_{X}(x_{1},y_{1})-\tau_{Y}^{2}\bar{\psi}_{Y}(x_{1},y_{1})+\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}\psi_{X,Y}(x_{1},y_{1}),

where
\psi_{X,Y}
is defined in (
8.1
), and

\displaystyle\bar{\psi}_{X}(x_{1},y_{1})

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{2\tau_{X}^{4}}\bigg[\big(\lVert x_{1}\rVert^{2}-\tr(\Sigma_{X})\big)\big(x_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}y_{1}-\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}\big)+2x_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}\Sigma_{XY}y_{1}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\kappa\tr(H_{[11]}\circ Q_{X})\bigg]+A_{2,X}(x_{1},y_{1}),

\displaystyle\bar{\psi}_{Y}(x_{1},y_{1})

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{2\tau_{Y}^{4}}\bigg[\big(\lVert y_{1}\rVert^{2}-\tr(\Sigma_{Y})\big)\big(x_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}y_{1}-\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}\big)+2x_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}\Sigma_{Y}y_{1}

(9.4)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\kappa\tr(H_{[22]}\circ Q_{Y})\bigg]A_{2,Y}(x_{1},y_{1}).

Here
Q_{X},Q_{Y},A_{2,X},A_{2,Y}
are defined in Lemma
8.1
. Using
\bar{g}_{1},\bar{R}_{1}
defined above, we may expand
g_{1}
into the sum of main and residual terms as follows.

Lemma 9.3
.

The following expansion holds:

(9.5)

\displaystyle g_{1}(x_{1},y_{1})=\bar{g}_{1}(x_{1},y_{1})+\frac{1}{2\tau_{X}\tau_{Y}}\big(\bar{R}_{1}(x_{1},y_{1})-\mathbb{E}\bar{R}_{1}(X_{1},Y_{1})\big).

Now we will evaluate the variance of
\bar{g}_{1}
and
\bar{R}_{1}
. The variance of
\bar{g}_{1}
is given by the following.

Lemma 9.4
.

We have
\mathbb{E}\bar{g}_{1}^{2}(X,Y)=4^{-2}n\cdot\bar{\sigma}_{n,1}^{2}
, where
\bar{\sigma}_{n,1}^{2}
is given in Theorem
2.2
.

As mentioned above, the variance of
\bar{g}_{1}
as above involves terms with a negative sign that are contributed by the ‘correction terms’
\mathscr{A}_{1,X}(x_{1},y_{1}),\mathscr{A}_{1,Y}(x_{1},y_{1})
. These terms can be of the same order as the main terms. It is therefore important to have a lower bound on this quantity.

Lemma 9.5
.

(1)

Suppose
\lVert\Sigma^{-1}\rVert_{\op}\leq M
for some
M>1
. Then
\mathbb{E}\bar{g}_{1}^{2}(X,Y)\gtrsim\tau_{X}^{-2}\tau_{Y}^{-2}\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}
. If furthermore
\lVert\Sigma\rVert_{\op}\leq M
, then
\mathbb{E}\bar{g}_{1}^{2}(X,Y)\asymp\tau_{X}^{-2}\tau_{Y}^{-2}\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}
.

(2)

Suppose
X=Y
, and
\lVert\Sigma_{X}^{-1}\rVert_{\op}\leq M
for some
M>1
. Then
\mathbb{E}\bar{g}_{1}^{2}(X,X)\gtrsim\tau_{X}^{-8}\cdot p\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{4}
. If furthermore
\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{\op}\leq M
, then
\mathbb{E}\bar{g}_{1}^{2}(X,X)\asymp\tau_{X}^{-2}
.

The constants in
\gtrsim,\asymp
only depend on
M
and the distribution of
Z_{1}
via its excess kurtosis
\kappa
in Assumption
A
.

Lemma
9.5
above is an important result, showing that the negative contributions of the ‘correction terms’
\mathscr{A}_{1,X}(x_{1},y_{1}),\mathscr{A}_{1,Y}(x_{1},y_{1})
will not affect the order the variance
\bar{g}_{1}
. In other words, these terms will contribute a non-vanishing but small proportion of the main terms.

Next to the variance of the main term
\bar{g}_{1}
, an important step to obtain variance bound for the residual term
\bar{R}_{1}
is to obtain variance bounds for
\bar{\psi}_{X},\bar{\psi}_{Y}
defined in (
9.1
).

Lemma 9.6
.

Suppose that the spectrum of
\Sigma_{X},\Sigma_{Y}
is contained in
[1/M,M]
for some
M>1
. Then

\displaystyle\tau_{X}^{6}\var\big(\bar{\psi}_{X}(X,Y)\big)\bigvee\tau_{Y}^{6}\var\big(\bar{\psi}_{Y}(X,Y)\big)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M,Z_{1}}\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}.

Here the dependence of
\lesssim
on
Z_{1}
is via its Poincaré constant
c_{\ast}
and
\varepsilon_{0}
prescribed by Assumption
A
.

This variance bound plays an important role to keep the residual terms small when
\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}
is large. In particular, if one uses the vanilla versions
\psi_{X},\psi_{Y}
defined in (
8.1
), the right hand side of the above display scales as
\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4}
that would lead to essential difficulties in controlling the residuals. In other words, the reduction from
\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4}
to
\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}
is made possible by the ‘correction terms’
\mathscr{A}_{1,X}(x_{1},y_{1}),\mathscr{A}_{1,Y}(x_{1},y_{1})
that, in a certain sense, ‘center’ the vanilla versions
\psi_{X},\psi_{Y}
to reduce the variance.

Detailed proofs of Lemmas
9.2
-
9.6
are deferred to Appendix
D
. Now we are in a good position to prove Proposition
9.1
.

Proof of Proposition
9.1
.

By (
9.3
),

\displaystyle\var\big(\bar{R}_{1}(X_{1},Y_{1})\big)

\displaystyle\lesssim\tau_{X}^{4}\var\big(\bar{\psi}_{X}(X_{1},Y_{1})\big)+\tau_{Y}^{4}\var\big(\bar{\psi}_{Y}(X_{1},Y_{1})\big)+\tau_{X}^{4}\tau_{Y}^{4}\mathbb{E}\psi_{X,Y}^{2}(X_{1},Y_{1}).

The first two terms can be handled by Lemma
9.6
, while the last term can be bounded by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\psi_{X,Y}^{2}(X_{1},Y_{1})\lesssim_{M}\tau_{X}^{-4}\tau_{Y}^{-4}\bigg[\frac{\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}+\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4}}{(\tau_{X}\wedge\tau_{Y})^{2}}\bigwedge 1\bigg].

This follows by Proposition
8.2
-(2) and the simple bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\psi_{X,Y}^{2}(X_{1},Y_{1})\leq\mathbb{E}\bar{R}_{X}^{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}\bar{R}_{Y}^{2}\lesssim_{M}\tau_{X}^{-4}\tau_{Y}^{-4}

by using Lemma
7.5
. Summarizing the estimates, we have

\displaystyle\var\big(\bar{R}_{1}(X_{1},Y_{1})\big)\lesssim\frac{\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}}{(\tau_{X}\wedge\tau_{Y})^{2}}+\frac{\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4}}{(\tau_{X}\wedge\tau_{Y})^{2}}\bigwedge 1.

As
\var(g_{1})=(1\pm\varepsilon)\var(\bar{g}_{1})+\mathcal{O}\big(\varepsilon^{-1}\cdot\tau_{X}^{-2}\tau_{Y}^{-2}\var(\bar{R}_{1}({X,Y})\big)
for any
\varepsilon>0
, the proof is now complete by noting that

\displaystyle\frac{\var(\bar{R}_{1}({X,Y}))}{\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}\var(\bar{g}_{1})}\lesssim_{M}\frac{\frac{\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}}{(\tau_{X}\wedge\tau_{Y})^{2}}+\frac{\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4}}{(\tau_{X}\wedge\tau_{Y})^{2}}\bigwedge 1}{\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\tau_{X}\wedge\tau_{Y}},

using Lemma
9.5
in the first inequality.
∎

9.2.
Hoeffding decomposition: 2nd order

The goal of this subsection is to prove the following variance expansion for the second-order kernel associated with
k
.

Proposition 9.7
.

Suppose that the spectrum of
\Sigma
is contained in
[1/M,M]
for some
M>1
. For any
\varepsilon>0
, the second-order variance is given by

\displaystyle\binom{4}{2}^{2}\binom{n}{2}^{-1}\mathbb{E}g_{2}^{2}\big((X_{1},Y_{1}),(X_{2},Y_{2})\big)=(1\pm\varepsilon)\cdot\bar{\sigma}_{n,2}^{2}(X,Y)\cdot\Big[1+\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{1}{\varepsilon\cdot(\tau_{X}\wedge\tau_{Y})^{2}}\Big)\Big].

Here
\bar{\sigma}_{n,2}^{2}(X,Y)
is as defined in Theorem
2.2
, the constant in
\mathcal{O}
depends on
M
and the distribution of
Z_{1}
only via its Poincaré constant
c_{\ast}
, excess kurtosis
\kappa
, and
\varepsilon_{0}
prescribed by Assumption
A
. The claim remains valid with
X=Y
when the spectrum of
\Sigma_{X}=\Sigma_{Y}
is contained in
[1/M,M]
for some
M>1
.

The prove Proposition
9.7
, we will first get an expansion for
g_{2}
, which requires a calculation of
k_{2}
:

Lemma 9.8
.

The second order kernel is given by

\displaystyle k_{2}(z_{1},z_{2})=\mathbb{E}k(z_{1},z_{2},Z_{3},Z_{4})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6}\bigg[U(x_{1},x_{2})V(y_{1},y_{2})+2\mathbb{E}U(x_{1},X)V(y_{1},Y)+2\mathbb{E}U(x_{2},X)V(y_{2},Y)+\dcov^{2}(X,Y)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\mathbb{E}U(x_{1},X)V(y_{2},Y)-\mathbb{E}U(x_{2},X)V(y_{1},Y)\bigg].

We will use the above lemma to devise an expansion for
g_{2}
. In the first-order expansion in the previous subsection, we have seen that the approximation of
U,V
in (
6.2
) is
not
enough to get a precise variance expansion of
g_{1}
. Somewhat interestingly, as announced in Section
6
such approximation is good enough in the second-order expansion. Formally, let the ‘main term’ of
g_{2}
be defined by

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{2}\big((x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2})\big)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{6\tau_{X}\tau_{Y}}\bigg[\Big(x_{1}^{\top}x_{2}y_{1}^{\top}y_{2}-x_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}y_{1}-x_{2}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}y_{2}+\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}\Big)

(9.6)

\displaystyle\qquad-\Big(x_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}y_{2}+x_{2}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}y_{1}\Big)\bigg],

and the ‘residual term’ be defined by [recall the definitions of
\bar{R}_{X},\bar{R}_{Y}
after (
7.3
)]

\displaystyle\bar{R}_{2}\big((x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2})\big)

\displaystyle=-\tau_{Y}^{2}x_{1}^{\top}x_{2}\bar{R}_{Y}(y_{1},y_{2})-\tau_{X}^{2}y_{1}^{\top}y_{2}\bar{R}_{X}(x_{1},x_{2})+\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}\bar{R}_{X}(x_{1},x_{2})\bar{R}_{Y}(y_{1},y_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad-R_{1}(x_{1},y_{1})-R_{1}(x_{2},y_{2})-R_{1}(x_{1},y_{2})-R_{1}(x_{2},y_{1}),

with
R_{1}
defined by [recall the definitions of
\psi_{X},\psi_{Y},\psi_{X,Y}
in (
8.1
)]

(9.7)

\displaystyle R_{1}(x_{1},y_{1})\equiv-\tau_{X}^{2}\psi_{X}(x_{1},y_{1})-\tau_{Y}^{2}\psi_{Y}(x_{1},y_{1})+\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}\psi_{X,Y}(x_{1},y_{1}).

The following lemma gives an expansion of
g_{2}
into the sum of the main term
\bar{g}_{2}
and the centered residual term
\bar{R}_{2}
.

Lemma 9.9
.

The following expansion holds:

\displaystyle g_{2}\big((x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2})\big)

\displaystyle=\bar{g}_{2}\big((x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2})\big)+\frac{1}{6\tau_{X}\tau_{Y}}\Big[\bar{R}_{2}\big((x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2})\big)-\mathbb{E}\bar{R}_{2}\big((X_{1},Y_{1}),(X_{2},Y_{2})\big)\Big].

Proofs of the proceeding lemmas can be found in Appendix
D
. Using the above decomposition, we only need to compute the variance for the two terms
\bar{g}_{2}
,
\bar{R}_{2}
on the right hand side of the above display for the proof of Proposition
9.7
. Clearly the variance of
\bar{g}_{2}
can be evaluated by a book-keeping calculation, and the variance of
\bar{R}_{2}
can be handled by the residual estimates in Proposition
8.2
. The proof below illustrates the strength of the bounds obtained in Proposition
8.2
.

Proof of Proposition
9.7
.

First note that we may expand
36\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}\mathbb{E}\bar{g}_{2}^{2}\big((X_{1},Y_{1}),(X_{2},Y_{2})\big)
as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[X_{1}^{\top}X_{2}Y_{1}^{\top}Y_{2}-X_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}Y_{1}-X_{2}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}Y_{2}+\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}\Big]^{2}+\mathbb{E}\Big[X_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}Y_{2}+X_{2}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}Y_{1}\Big]^{2}\equiv E_{1}+E_{2}.

This follows as the cross term has expectation
0
due to the symmetry of the distribution of
(X,Y)
. After expansion, we have.

\displaystyle E_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}(X_{1}^{\top}X_{2}Y_{1}^{\top}Y_{2})^{2}+2\mathbb{E}(X_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}Y_{1})^{2}+\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4}-4\mathbb{E}(X_{1}^{\top}X_{2}Y_{1}^{\top}Y_{2}X_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}Y_{1})

\displaystyle\qquad+2\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4}+2\mathbb{E}(X_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}Y_{1}X_{2}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}Y_{2})-4\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4}.

The above terms can be calculated using Lemma
G.3
:

•

The first term is

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(X_{1}^{\top}X_{2}Y_{1}^{\top}Y_{2})^{2}

\displaystyle=2\Big(\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4}+\tr(\Sigma_{XY}\Sigma_{YX}\Sigma_{XY}\Sigma_{YX})+2\tr(\Sigma_{XY}\Sigma_{Y}\Sigma_{YX}\Sigma_{X})\Big)

\displaystyle+\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{2}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}^{2}+2\kappa\cdot\big[2\tr(G_{[12]}\circ G_{[12]})+\tr(H_{[11]}^{2}\circ H_{[22]}^{2})\big]+\kappa^{2}\cdot\lVert H_{[11]}\circ H_{[22]}\rVert_{F}^{2}.

•

The second term is

\displaystyle 2\mathbb{E}(X_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}Y_{1})^{2}=2\Big(\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4}+\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\Sigma_{YX}\rVert_{F}^{2}+\tr(\Sigma_{XY}\Sigma_{Y}\Sigma_{YX}\Sigma_{X})+\kappa\cdot\tr(G_{[12]}\circ G_{[12]})\Big).

•

The fourth term is

\displaystyle-4\mathbb{E}(X_{1}^{\top}X_{2}Y_{1}^{\top}Y_{2}X_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}Y_{1})=-4\mathbb{E}(X_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}Y_{1})^{2}

\displaystyle=-4\Big(\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4}+\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\Sigma_{YX}\rVert_{F}^{2}+\tr(\Sigma_{XY}\Sigma_{Y}\Sigma_{YX}\Sigma_{X})+\kappa\cdot\tr(G_{[12]}\circ G_{[12]})\Big).

•

The sixth term is
2\mathbb{E}(X_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}Y_{1}X_{2}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}Y_{2})=2\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4}
.

In summary, we have

\displaystyle E_{1}

\displaystyle=\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{2}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}^{2}+\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4}+2\tr(\Sigma_{XY}\Sigma_{Y}\Sigma_{YX}\Sigma_{X})

\displaystyle+2\kappa\cdot\big(\tr(G_{[12]}\circ G_{[12]})+\tr(H_{[11]}^{2}\circ H_{[22]}^{2})\big)+\kappa^{2}\cdot\lVert H_{[11]}\circ H_{[22]}\rVert_{F}^{2}.

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle E_{2}

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{E}(X_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}Y_{2})^{2}+2\mathbb{E}(X_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}Y_{2}X_{2}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}Y_{1})=2\tr(\Sigma_{XY}\Sigma_{Y}\Sigma_{YX}\Sigma_{X})+2\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\Sigma_{YX}\rVert_{F}^{2}.

Combining the identities and applying Lemmas
7.2
and
G.5
yields that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bar{g}_{2}^{2}\big((X_{1},Y_{1}),(X_{2},Y_{2})\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{36\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}}\Big[\Big(\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{2}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}^{2}+4\tr(\Sigma_{XY}\Sigma_{Y}\Sigma_{YX}\Sigma_{X})+\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4}+2\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\Sigma_{YX}\rVert_{F}^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad+2\kappa\cdot\big(\tr(G_{[12]}\circ G_{[12]})+\tr(H_{[11]}^{2}\circ H_{[22]}^{2})\big)+\kappa^{2}\cdot\lVert H_{[11]}\circ H_{[22]}\rVert_{F}^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{36\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}}\Big(\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{2}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}^{2}+\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4})\bigg[1+\mathcal{O}_{M,\kappa}\Big(\frac{1}{(\tau_{X}\wedge\tau_{Y})^{2}}\Big)\bigg].

For the residual term, it can be bounded as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bar{R}_{2}^{2}\big((X_{1},Y_{1}),(X_{2},Y_{2})\big)

\displaystyle\lesssim\tau_{Y}^{4}\mathbb{E}^{1/2}(X_{1}^{\top}X_{2})^{4}\cdot\mathbb{E}^{1/2}\bar{R}_{Y}^{4}+\tau_{X}^{4}\mathbb{E}^{1/2}(Y_{1}^{\top}Y_{2})^{4}\cdot\mathbb{E}^{1/2}\bar{R}_{X}^{4}

\displaystyle\qquad+\tau_{X}^{4}\tau_{Y}^{4}\mathbb{E}^{1/2}\bar{R}_{X}^{4}\cdot\mathbb{E}^{1/2}\bar{R}_{Y}^{4}+\mathbb{E}R_{1}^{2}.

Using Proposition
8.2
-(2), it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}R_{1}^{2}\lesssim\tau_{X}^{4}\mathbb{E}\psi_{X}^{2}+\tau_{Y}^{4}\mathbb{E}\psi_{Y}^{2}+\tau_{X}^{4}\tau_{Y}^{4}\mathbb{E}\psi_{X,Y}^{2}\lesssim_{M}\frac{\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}(1\vee\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2})}{(\tau_{X}\wedge\tau_{Y})^{2}}.

This, combined with Lemma
7.5
-(2) and an easy calculation that
\mathbb{E}(X_{1}^{\top}X_{2})^{4}\lesssim\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{4}
and
\mathbb{E}(Y_{1}^{\top}Y_{2})^{4}\lesssim\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}^{4}
, shows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bar{R}_{2}^{2}\big((X_{1},Y_{1}),(X_{2},Y_{2})\big)\lesssim_{M}\tau_{Y}^{4}\cdot\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{2}\cdot\tau_{Y}^{-8}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}^{4}+\tau_{X}^{4}\cdot\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}^{2}\cdot\tau_{X}^{-8}\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{4}

\displaystyle\qquad+\tau_{X}^{4}\tau_{Y}^{4}\cdot\tau_{Y}^{-8}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}^{4}\cdot\tau_{X}^{-8}\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{4}+\frac{\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}(1\vee\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2})}{(\tau_{X}\wedge\tau_{Y})^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M}\frac{\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{2}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}^{2}+\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}(1\vee\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2})}{(\tau_{X}\wedge\tau_{Y})^{2}}.

As
\var(g_{2})=(1\pm\varepsilon)\var(\bar{g}_{2})+\mathcal{O}\big(\varepsilon^{-1}\cdot\tau_{X}^{-2}\tau_{Y}^{-2}\var(\bar{R}_{2}({X,Y})\big)
for any
\varepsilon>0
, the proof is now complete by noting that

\displaystyle\frac{\var\big(\bar{R}_{2}({X,Y})\big)}{\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}\var(\bar{g}_{2})}\lesssim_{M}\frac{\frac{\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{2}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}^{2}+\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}+\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4}}{(\tau_{X}\wedge\tau_{Y})^{2}}}{\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{2}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}^{2}+\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4}}\lesssim_{M}\frac{1}{(\tau_{X}\wedge\tau_{Y})^{2}},

as desired.
∎

9.3.
Hoeffding decomposition: higher orders

The goal of this section is to prove the following.

Proposition 9.10
.

Suppose that the spectrum of
\Sigma
is contained in
[1/M,M]
for some
M>1
. Then the third- and fourth- order variance are bounded by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}g_{3}^{2}+\mathbb{E}g_{4}^{2}\lesssim\tau_{X}^{-2}\tau_{Y}^{-2}\Big(\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{2}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}^{2}+\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}+\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4}\Big)\lesssim\mathbb{E}g_{1}^{2}+\mathbb{E}g_{2}^{2}.

Here the constants in
\lesssim
depend on
M
and the distribution of
Z_{1}
only via its Poincaré constant
c_{\ast}
, excess kurtosis
\kappa
, and
\varepsilon_{0}
prescribed by Assumption
A
. The claims remain valid with
X=Y
when the spectrum of
\Sigma_{X}=\Sigma_{Y}
is contained in
[1/M,M]
for some
M>1
.

To prove this proposition, we need to evaluate
k_{3}
and
k_{4}
.
k_{4}=k
is already given by Proposition
2.1
, so we only need to compute
k_{3}
as follows.

Lemma 9.11
.

The third order kernel is given by

\displaystyle k_{3}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=\mathbb{E}k(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},Z_{4})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{12}\bigg[2\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}\leq 3}U(x_{i_{1}},x_{i_{2}})V(y_{i_{1}},y_{i_{2}})+2\sum_{1\leq i\leq 3}\mathbb{E}U(X,x_{i})V(Y,y_{i})

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})\in\sigma(1,2,3)}U(x_{i_{1}},x_{i_{2}})V(y_{i_{1}},y_{i_{3}})-\sum_{1\leq i_{1}\neq i_{2}\leq 3}\mathbb{E}U(X,x_{i_{1}})V(Y,y_{i_{2}})\bigg].

The proof of the above lemma can be found in Appendix
D
.

Proof of Proposition
9.10
.

For the second moment of
g_{3}
, we each term in its definition (
9
) can be bounded as follows:

•

First we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}k_{3}^{2}\big((X_{1},Y_{1}),(X_{2},Y_{2}),(X_{3},Y_{3})\big)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}^{1/2}U^{4}(X_{1},X_{2})\cdot\mathbb{E}^{1/2}V^{4}(Y_{1},Y_{2})\lesssim\tau_{X}^{-2}\tau_{Y}^{-2}\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{2}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}^{2}.

The last inequality follows as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}U^{4}(X_{1},X_{2})

\displaystyle\lesssim\tau_{X}^{-4}\Big(\mathbb{E}(X_{1}^{\top}X_{2})^{4}+\tau_{X}^{8}\mathbb{E}\bar{R}_{X}^{4}(X_{1},X_{2})\Big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{\lesssim}}\tau_{X}^{-4}\big(\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{4}+\tau_{X}^{-8}\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{8}\big)\lesssim\tau_{X}^{-4}\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{4},

and similarly
\mathbb{E}V^{4}(Y_{1},Y_{2})\lesssim\tau_{Y}^{-4}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}^{4}
, using Lemma
7.5
in
(*)
.

•

By Theorem
8.4
,

\displaystyle\big(\dcov^{2}(X,Y)\big)^{2}\lesssim_{M}\tau_{X}^{-2}\tau_{Y}^{-2}\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4}.

•

By Proposition
9.1
and Lemma
G.5
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}g_{1}^{2}(X_{1},Y_{1})

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M}\tau_{X}^{-2}\tau_{Y}^{-2}\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}.

•

Byy Proposition
9.7
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}g_{2}^{2}\big((X_{1},Y_{1}),(X_{2},Y_{2})\big)\lesssim_{M}\tau_{X}^{-2}\tau_{Y}^{-2}\big(\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{2}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}^{2}+\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4}\big).

Collecting the above bounds and using the variance lower bound in Lemma
9.5
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}g_{3}^{2}\lesssim_{M}\tau_{X}^{-2}\tau_{Y}^{-2}\Big(\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{2}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}^{2}+\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}+\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4}\Big)\lesssim_{M}\mathbb{E}g_{1}^{2}+\mathbb{E}g_{2}^{2}.

The second moment bound for
g_{4}
can be obtained in a similar way so we omit the proof.
∎

9.4.
Variance expansion

With the groundwork laid above, we are now able to prove the following variance expansion formula.

Theorem 9.12
.

Suppose that the spectrum of
\Sigma
is contained in
[1/M,M]
for some
M>1
. Then

\displaystyle\bigg\lvert\frac{\var\big(\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})\big)}{\bar{\sigma}_{n}^{2}(X,Y)}-1\bigg\rvert\lesssim n^{-1/2}+(p\wedge q)^{-1/4}.

Here
\bar{\sigma}_{n}^{2}(X,Y)
is defined in Theorem
2.2
, and the constant in
\lesssim
depends on
M
and the distribution of
Z_{1}
only via its Poincaré constant
c_{\ast}
, excess kurtosis
\kappa
, and
\varepsilon_{0}
prescribed by Assumption
A
. The claims remain valid with
X=Y
when the spectrum of
\Sigma_{X}=\Sigma_{Y}
is contained in
[1/M,M]
for some
M>1
.

Proof.

By Hoeffding decomposition
\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})=\sum_{c=0}^{4}\binom{4}{c}U_{n}(g_{c})
, so

\displaystyle\sigma_{\Sigma}^{2}\equiv\var_{\Sigma}\big(\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})\big)=\sum_{c=1}^{4}\binom{4}{c}^{2}\binom{n}{c}^{-1}\mathbb{E}g_{c}^{2}.

Now we may apply Propositions
9.1
,
9.7
, and
9.10
to conclude that the left hand side of the desired inequality is bounded by

\displaystyle\inf_{\varepsilon>0}\bigg\lvert(1+\varepsilon)\bigg(1+\frac{\mathcal{O}_{M}(n^{-1}+(p\wedge q)^{-1/2})}{\varepsilon}\bigg)-1\bigg\rvert\asymp n^{-1/2}+(p\wedge q)^{-1/4}.

The claim follows.
∎

10.
Normal approximation of truncated
\dcov_{\ast}^{2}

Let
\bar{g}_{0}\equiv\mathbb{E}\dcov_{*}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})=\dcov^{2}(X,Y)
. Recall
\bar{g}_{1},\bar{g}_{2}
defined in (
9.2
) and (
9.2
). Define the truncated sample distance covariance:

(10.1)

\displaystyle\bar{T}_{n}\big(\bm{X},\bm{Y}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{c=0}^{2}\binom{4}{c}U_{n}(\bar{g}_{c}).

The goal of this section is to prove the following non-null central limit theorem for
\bar{T}_{n}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})
.

Theorem 10.1
.

Suppose that the spectrum of
\Sigma
lies in
[1/M,M]
for some
M>1
. Then there exists some
C=C(M,Z_{1})>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{err}_{n}\equiv d_{\mathrm{Kol}}\bigg(\frac{\bar{T}_{n}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})-\mathbb{E}\bar{T}_{n}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})}{\var^{1/2}(\bar{T}_{n}(\bm{X},\bm{Y}))},\mathcal{N}(0,1)\bigg)\leq C\bigg(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{1}{pq}\bigg)^{1/4}.

Here
\var(\bar{T}_{n}(\bm{X},\bm{Y}))=\bar{\sigma}_{n}^{2}(X,Y)
is defined in Theorem
2.2
, and
C
depends on
Z_{1}
only via its Poincaré constant
c_{\ast}
, excess kurtosis
\kappa
, and
\varepsilon_{0}
prescribed by Assumption
A
.

The major tool to prove the CLT in Theorem
10.1
is the following discrete second-order Poincaré inequality proved by Chatterjee
[
Cha08
]
.

Lemma 10.2

(Discrete second-order Poincaré inequality)
.

Let
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
be a vector of independent
\mathcal{X}
-valued random variables, and
X^{\prime}=(X_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,X_{n}^{\prime})
be an independent copy of
X
. For any
A\subset[n]
, define the random variable

\displaystyle X_{i}^{A}\equiv\begin{cases}X_{i}^{\prime},&\text{if }i\in A,\\
X_{i},&\text{if }i\notin A.\end{cases}

Define
\Delta_{j}f\equiv f(X)-f(X^{\{j\}})
,
T_{A}\equiv\sum_{j\notin A}\Delta_{j}f(X)\Delta_{j}f(X^{A})
, and

\displaystyle T\equiv\frac{1}{2}\sum_{A\subsetneq[n]}\frac{T_{A}}{{n\choose|A|}(n-|A|)}.

Then with
W\equiv f(X)
admitting finite variance
\sigma^{2}
,

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{Kol}}\bigg(\frac{W-\mathbb{E}(W)}{\var^{1/2}(W)},\mathcal{N}(0,1)\bigg)\leq 2\bigg[\frac{\var^{1/2}\big(\mathbb{E}(T|W)\big)}{\sigma^{2}}+\frac{1}{2\sigma^{3}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}|\Delta_{j}f(X)|^{3}\bigg]^{1/2}.

Proof.

This follows from
[
Cha08
, Theorem 2.2]
and Lemma
G.2
.
∎

We start with the following decomposition of
\bar{T}_{n}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})
. Its proof will be presented in Appendix
E.1
.

Lemma 10.3
.

Let

\displaystyle\psi_{1}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})

\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{I_{n}^{2}}\big(X_{i_{1}}^{\top}X_{i_{2}}Y_{i_{1}}^{\top}Y_{i_{2}}-\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}\big),

\displaystyle\psi_{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})

\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{i\neq j}\big(X_{i}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}Y_{j}+X_{j}^{\top}\Sigma_{XY}Y_{i}\big),

\displaystyle\psi_{3}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})

\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big[\frac{\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}}{\tau_{X}^{2}}\big(\lVert X_{i}\rVert^{2}-\tr(\Sigma_{X})\big)+\frac{\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}}{\tau_{Y}^{2}}\big(\lVert Y_{i}\rVert^{2}-\tr(\Sigma_{Y})\big)\Big].

Then

\displaystyle\bar{T}_{n}\big(\bm{X},\bm{Y}\big)=\dcov^{2}(X,Y)+\frac{1}{\tau_{X}\tau_{Y}\cdot 2\binom{n}{2}}\Big(\psi_{1}\big(\bm{X},\bm{Y}\big)-\psi_{2}\big(\bm{X},\bm{Y}\big)\Big)-\frac{2}{\tau_{X}\tau_{Y}n}\psi_{3}(\bm{X},\bm{Y}).

Outline of the proof of Theorem
10.1
.

Define
T_{\psi_{1}}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})
-
T_{\psi_{3}}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})
and
\Delta_{i}\psi_{1}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})
-
\Delta_{i}\psi_{3}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})
as in the discrete second-order Poincaré inequality (cf. Lemma
10.2
). The following three propositions give variance and third moment bounds for these quantities.

Proposition 10.4

(Analysis of
\psi_{1}
)
.

Assume the conditions in Theorem
10.1
. Then the following hold:

(1)

(Variance bound)

\displaystyle\var\big[\mathbb{E}(T_{\psi_{1}}|\bm{X},\bm{Y})\big]

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{3}\cdot\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{4}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}^{4}+n^{4}\cdot(1\vee\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2})\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{2}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+n^{5}\cdot(1\vee\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2})\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}.

(2)

(Third moment bound)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}|\Delta_{i}\psi_{1}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})|^{3}\lesssim n^{5/2}\tr^{3/2}(\Sigma_{X})\tr^{3/2}(\Sigma_{Y})+n^{4}\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{3}.

The constants in
\lesssim
depend on
M
and the distribution of
Z_{1}
only.

Proposition 10.5

(Analysis of
\psi_{2}
)
.

Assume the conditions in Theorem
10.1
. Then the following hold.

(1)

\var\big[\mathbb{E}(T_{\psi_{2}}|\bm{X},\bm{Y})\big]\lesssim n^{4}\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4}
.

(2)

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}|\Delta_{j}\psi_{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})|^{3}\lesssim n^{5/2}\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{3}
.

The constants in
\lesssim
depend on
M
and the distribution of
Z_{1}
only.

Proposition 10.6

(Analysis of
\psi_{3}
)
.

Assume the conditions in Theorem
10.1
. Then the following hold.

(1)

\var\big[\mathbb{E}(T_{\psi_{3}}|\bm{X},\bm{Y})\big]\lesssim n\cdot\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{8}(\tau_{X}^{-4}+\tau_{Y}^{-4})
.

(2)

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}|\Delta_{i}\psi_{3}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})|^{3}\lesssim n\cdot\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{6}(\tau_{X}^{-3}+\tau_{Y}^{-3})
.

The constants in
\lesssim
depend on
M
and the distribution of
Z_{1}
only.

The proofs of these propositions will be detailed in Appendix
E
. By the proceeding propositions and Lemma
G.5
, we have

\displaystyle D_{1}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\var^{1/2}\big(\mathbb{E}(T_{\psi_{1}}|\bm{X},\bm{Y})\big)+\var^{1/2}\big(\mathbb{E}(T_{\psi_{2}}|\bm{X},\bm{Y})\big)}{n^{4}\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}}+\frac{\var^{1/2}\big(\mathbb{E}(T_{\psi_{3}}|\bm{X},\bm{Y})\big)}{n^{2}\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M}\bigg[\frac{1}{n^{5/2}}+\frac{1\vee\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}}{n^{2}\tau_{X}\tau_{Y}}+\frac{(1\vee\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F})\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}}{n^{3/2}\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}}\bigg]+\frac{\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4}}{n^{3/2}\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}(\tau_{X}^{2}\wedge\tau_{Y}^{2})}

\displaystyle\asymp_{M}\frac{1}{n^{2}\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}}\Big[n^{-1/2}\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}+\tau_{X}\tau_{Y}(1\vee\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F})+n^{1/2}(1\vee\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F})\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}\Big],

and

\displaystyle D_{2}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}|\Delta_{i}\psi_{1}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})|^{3}+\mathbb{E}|\Delta_{i}\psi_{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})|^{3}}{n^{6}\tau_{X}^{3}\tau_{Y}^{3}}+\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}|\Delta_{i}\psi_{3}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})|^{3}}{n^{3}\tau_{X}^{3}\tau_{Y}^{3}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M}\bigg[\frac{1}{n^{7/2}}+\frac{\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{3}}{n^{2}\tau_{X}^{3}\tau_{Y}^{3}}\bigg]+\frac{n\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{6}(\tau_{X}^{-3}+\tau_{Y}^{-3})}{n^{3}\tau_{X}^{3}\tau_{Y}^{3}}

\displaystyle\asymp_{M}\frac{1}{n^{3}\tau_{X}^{3}\tau_{Y}^{3}}\Big[n^{-1/2}\tau_{X}^{3}\tau_{Y}^{3}+n\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{3}\Big].

Using Theorem
9.12
with the lower bound Lemma
9.5
, we have

(10.2)

\displaystyle\bar{\sigma}_{n}^{2}=\var\big(\bar{T}_{n}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})\big)\gtrsim_{M}\frac{1}{n^{2}\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}}\Big[n\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}+\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}+\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4}\Big].

This entails that

\displaystyle\frac{D_{1}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M}\frac{n^{-1/2}\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}+\tau_{X}\tau_{Y}(1\vee\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F})+n^{1/2}(1\vee\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F})\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}}{n\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}+\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}+\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{n^{1/2}}+\frac{1}{\tau_{X}\tau_{Y}}+\frac{\tau_{X}\tau_{Y}\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}}{n\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}+\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}}+\frac{n^{1/2}\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}}{n\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}+\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}}\asymp\frac{1}{n^{1/2}}+\frac{1}{\tau_{X}\tau_{Y}},

\displaystyle\frac{D_{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{3}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M}\frac{n^{-1/2}\tau_{X}^{3}\tau_{Y}^{3}+n\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{3}}{n^{3/2}\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{3}+\tau_{X}^{3}\tau_{Y}^{3}+\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{6}}\lesssim\frac{1}{n^{1/2}}.

The claim follows by using Lemma
10.2
in the form

\displaystyle\mathrm{err}_{n}\leq C\cdot\bigg[\frac{D_{1}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}+\frac{D_{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{3}}\bigg]^{1/2},

as desired.
∎

As outlined above, the major step in the proof is to obtain good enough variance and third moment bounds for
\mathbb{E}[T_{\psi_{1}}|\bm{X},\bm{Y}]
-
\mathbb{E}[T_{\psi_{3}}|\bm{X},\bm{Y}]
and
\Delta_{i}\psi_{1}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})
-
\Delta_{i}\psi_{3}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})
, as claimed in Propositions
10.4
-
10.6
. The proofs to these propositions are fairly delicate and involved. The most complicated case appears to be the control for
\mathbb{E}[T_{\psi_{1}}|\bm{X},\bm{Y}],\Delta_{i}\psi_{1}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})
associated with the first term
\psi_{1}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})
due to its highest polynomial order by definition. The structure of the bounds in Propositions
10.4
-
10.6
also reveals a careful balance among the power in the terms
n,\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F},\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}
. Such a balance turns out to be crucial to reach the announced error bound in Theorem
10.1
that requires no more than a bounded spectrum condition. See Appendix
E
for proof details.

11.
Proof of Theorem
2.2

(
Step 1
) By definition of
\bar{T}_{n}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})
in (
10.1
), we have

\displaystyle\Delta_{n}\equiv\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})-\bar{T}_{n}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})=\sum_{c=1}^{2}U_{n}(g_{c}-\bar{g}_{c})+\sum_{c=3}^{4}U_{n}(g_{c}).

This means

\displaystyle\frac{\var(\Delta_{n})}{\bar{\sigma}_{n}^{2}(X,Y)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{c=1}^{2}\frac{\var\big[U_{n}\big(g_{c}-\bar{g}_{c}\big)\big]}{\bar{\sigma}_{n}^{2}(X,Y)}+\sum_{c=3}^{4}\frac{\var\big[U_{n}(g_{c})\big]}{\bar{\sigma}_{n}^{2}(X,Y)}.

For
c=1
, by Lemma
9.3
and the proof of Proposition
9.1
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\var\big[U_{n}(g_{1}-\bar{g}_{1})\big]}{\bar{\sigma}_{n}^{2}(X,Y)}\asymp\frac{\var(\bar{R}_{1}(X,Y))}{n\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}\bar{\sigma}_{n}^{2}(X,Y)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\var(\bar{R}_{1})}{\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}\var(\bar{g}_{1})}\lesssim_{M}\frac{1}{\tau_{X}\wedge\tau_{Y}}.

For
c=2
, by Lemma
9.9
and the proof of Proposition
9.7
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\var\big[U_{n}(g_{2}-\bar{g}_{2})\big]}{\bar{\sigma}_{n}^{2}(X,Y)}\lesssim_{M}\frac{\var(\bar{R}_{2})}{n^{2}\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}\bar{\sigma}_{n}^{2}(X,Y)}\lesssim_{M}\frac{\var(\bar{R}_{2})}{\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}\var(\bar{g}_{2})}\lesssim_{M}\frac{1}{(\tau_{X}\wedge\tau_{Y})^{2}}.

For
c=3,4
, the proof of Proposition
9.10
yields that

\displaystyle\var\big[U_{n}(g_{3})\big]+\var\big[U_{n}(g_{4})\big]\lesssim_{M}\frac{1}{n^{3}\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}}\big(\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{2}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}^{2}+\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}+\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4}\big),

so using the variance lower bound in (
10.2
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\var\big[U_{n}(g_{3})\big]+\var\big[U_{n}(g_{4})\big]}{\bar{\sigma}_{n}^{2}(X,Y)}\lesssim_{M}\frac{1}{n}.

Collecting the bounds, we have

(11.1)

\displaystyle\frac{\var(\Delta_{n})}{\bar{\sigma}_{n}^{2}(X,Y)}\lesssim_{M}\frac{1}{n}+\frac{1}{\tau_{X}\wedge\tau_{Y}}.

(
Step 2
) First consider normalization by
\bar{\sigma}_{n}(X,Y)=\var^{1/2}(\bar{T}_{n}(\bm{X},\bm{Y}))
. Using the decomposition

\displaystyle\bar{L}_{n}\equiv\frac{\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})-\dcov^{2}(X,Y)}{\bar{\sigma}_{n}(X,Y)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{n}}{\bar{\sigma}_{n}(X,Y)}+\frac{\bar{T}_{n}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})-\mathbb{E}\bar{T}_{n}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})}{\var^{1/2}(\bar{T}_{n}(\bm{X},\bm{Y}))},

by Lemma
G.1
and Theorem
10.1
(note that
\mathbb{E}\Delta_{n}=0
),

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{Kol}}\bigg(\frac{\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})-\dcov^{2}(X,Y)}{\bar{\sigma}_{n}(X,Y)},\mathcal{N}(0,1)\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq d_{\mathrm{Kol}}\bigg(\frac{\bar{T}_{n}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})-\mathbb{E}\bar{T}_{n}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})}{\var^{1/2}(\bar{T}_{n}(\bm{X},\bm{Y}))},\mathcal{N}(0,1)\bigg)+2\bigg(\frac{\var(\Delta_{n})}{\bar{\sigma}_{n}^{2}(X,Y)}\bigg)^{1/3}\lesssim_{M}\bigg(\frac{1}{n\wedge p\wedge q}\bigg)^{1/6}.

Next, with the normalization
\var^{1/2}(\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y}))
, consider the decomposition

\displaystyle\frac{\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})-\dcov^{2}(X,Y)}{\var^{1/2}(\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y}))}

\displaystyle=\bar{L}_{n}+\bar{L}_{n}\bigg(\frac{\bar{\sigma}_{n}(X,Y)}{\var^{1/2}(\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y}))}-1\bigg)\equiv\bar{L}_{n}+\bar{\Delta}_{n}.

By Theorem
9.12
,
\var(\bar{\Delta}_{n})\lesssim_{M}n^{-1}+(p\wedge q)^{-1/2}
. The claim now follows by invoking Lemma
G.1
.∎

12.
Proof of Theorem
2.7

We write
A(\Sigma)
as
A
for simplicity in the proof. Let

\displaystyle m_{0}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}}{\tau_{X}\tau_{Y}},\quad\sigma_{0}^{2}\equiv\frac{2\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{2}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}^{2}}{n^{2}\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}}.

Then with
\bar{\sigma}_{n}\equiv\bar{\sigma}_{n}(X,Y)
in the proof,

\displaystyle\frac{\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})-m_{0}}{\sigma_{0}}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})-\dcov^{2}(X,Y)}{\bar{\sigma}_{n}}+\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2},

where

\displaystyle\Delta_{1}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})-\dcov^{2}(X,Y)}{\bar{\sigma}_{n}}\bigg(\frac{\bar{\sigma}_{n}}{\sigma_{0}}-1\bigg),\quad\Delta_{2}\equiv\frac{\dcov^{2}(X,Y)-m_{0}}{\sigma_{0}}.

By Lemma
G.1
and Theorem
2.2
,

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{Kol}}\bigg(\frac{\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})-m_{0}}{\sigma_{0}},\mathcal{N}(0,1)\bigg)\leq C_{M}\bigg(\frac{1}{n\wedge p\wedge q}\bigg)^{1/6}+2\var^{1/3}(\Delta_{1})+\lvert\Delta_{2}\rvert.

To give a variance bound for
\Delta_{1}
, first by using Lemma
G.5
, it follows that

\displaystyle\bar{\sigma}_{n}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{M}\bigg(\frac{\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}}{n\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}}+\frac{\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{4}}{n\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}(n\wedge p\wedge q)}\bigg)+\frac{2\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{2}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}^{2}}{n^{2}\tau_{X}^{2}\tau_{Y}^{2}}.

So with
\rho(A)\equiv A/(n\wedge p\wedge q)^{1/2}
,

(12.1)

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{\sigma}_{n}^{2}}{\sigma_{0}^{2}}

\displaystyle=1+\mathcal{O}_{M}\bigg(\frac{A}{(pq)^{1/2}}+\frac{A^{2}}{n(n\wedge p\wedge q)}\bigg)=1+\mathcal{O}_{M}\big(\rho(A)+\rho^{2}(A)\big).

Therefore combined with Theorem
9.12
, we have

\displaystyle\var\big(\Delta_{1}\big)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\frac{\bar{\sigma}_{n}}{\sigma_{0}}-1\bigg)^{2}\cdot\frac{\var\big(\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})\big)}{\bar{\sigma}_{n}^{2}}\lesssim_{M}\big(\rho(A)+\rho^{2}(A)\big)^{2}.

On the other hand, by Theorem
8.4
we have

\displaystyle\lvert\Delta_{2}\rvert

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M}\frac{A}{(p\wedge q)^{1/2}}\lesssim\rho(A).

The claim follows by collecting the estimates and using
1\wedge t\lesssim 1\wedge t^{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in(0,1)
.∎

13.
Proof of Theorem
3.1

Lemma 13.1
.

Suppose that the spectrum of
\Sigma
is contained in
[1/M,M]
for some
M>1
. Then with
\Delta_{X}
and
\Delta_{Y}
defined by

\displaystyle\frac{\dcov_{\ast}(\bm{X})}{\dcov(X)}\equiv 1+\Delta_{X},\quad\frac{\dcov_{\ast}(\bm{Y})}{\dcov(Y)}\equiv 1+\Delta_{Y},

we have
\mathbb{E}\Delta_{X}^{2}\vee\mathbb{E}\Delta_{Y}^{2}\leq C(n\wedge p\wedge q)^{-2}
for some constant
C=C(M)>0
.

Proof.

By mean and variance expansions in Theorems
8.4
and
9.12
,

\displaystyle\dcov^{2}(X)=\frac{\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{2}}{\tau_{X}^{2}}\Big[1+\mathcal{O}_{M}(\tau_{X}^{-1})\Big],\quad\var\big(\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X})\big)\lesssim_{M}\frac{\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{2}}{n\tau_{X}^{4}}+\frac{\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{4}}{n^{2}\tau_{X}^{4}}.

Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Delta_{X}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X})-\dcov^{2}(X)}{\dcov(X)(\dcov_{*}(\bm{X})+\dcov(X))}\Big]^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\dcov^{-4}(X)\cdot\mathbb{E}\Big(\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X})-\dcov^{2}(X)\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle=\dcov^{-4}(X)\cdot\var\big(\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X})\big)\asymp_{M}(n\tau_{X}^{2})^{-1}+n^{-2}\lesssim_{M}(n\wedge p\wedge q)^{-2}.

Similar bounds can be derived for
\Delta_{Y}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Let
d_{X,Y}^{2}\equiv\dcov^{2}(X)\cdot\dcov^{2}(Y)
, and
\Delta_{d}
be defined by
d_{X,Y}\big/\big(\frac{\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}}{\tau_{X}}\cdot\frac{\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}}{\tau_{Y}}\big)=1+\Delta_{d}
. Then by Theorem
8.4
,
\Delta_{d}=\mathcal{O}_{M}\big((\tau_{X}\wedge\tau_{Y})^{-1}\big)
. Recall the definitions of (random)
\Delta_{X}
and
\Delta_{Y}
in Lemma
13.1
, and note that by definition
\Delta_{d},\Delta_{X},\Delta_{Y}\geq-1
. For any
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{n}(t)

\displaystyle\equiv\mathbb{P}\bigg(\frac{n\cdot\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})}{\sqrt{2\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X})\cdot\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{Y})}}\leq t\bigg)=\mathbb{P}\bigg(\frac{n\cdot\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})}{\sqrt{2}d_{X,Y}}\leq t(1+\Delta_{X})(1+\Delta_{Y})\bigg)

(13.1)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigg(\frac{n\cdot\tau_{X}\tau_{Y}\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})}{\sqrt{2}\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}}\leq t(1+\Delta_{X})(1+\Delta_{Y})(1+\Delta_{d})\bigg).

Let
\Delta_{1}
be defined by
(1+\Delta_{X})(1+\Delta_{Y})(1+\Delta_{d})=1+\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{d}
. Then Lemma
13.1
yields that
\mathbb{E}\Delta_{1}^{2}\lesssim_{M}(n\wedge p\wedge q)^{-2}
. From (
13
), it holds for any
\delta_{1},\delta_{d}\in(0,1/2]
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{n}(t)\leq\mathbb{P}\bigg(\frac{n\cdot\tau_{X}\tau_{Y}\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})}{\sqrt{2}\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}}\leq t(1+\delta_{1}+\delta_{d})\bigg)+\mathbb{P}(|\Delta_{1}|\geq\delta_{1})+\bm{1}_{|\Delta_{d}|\geq\delta_{d}}

(13.2)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\bigg(\frac{n\cdot\tau_{X}\tau_{Y}\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})}{\sqrt{2}\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}}\leq t(1+\delta_{1}+\delta_{d})\bigg)+\frac{C_{M}(n\wedge p\wedge q)^{-2}}{\delta_{1}^{2}}+\bm{1}_{|\Delta_{d}|\geq\delta_{d}},

using the bound on
\mathbb{E}\Delta_{1}^{2}
in the second inequality. Next we give two estimates for the upper bound of
\mathfrak{p}_{n}(t)
useful for different magnitudes of
A\equiv A(\Sigma)
.

(Estimate 1)
. By the local central limit theorem in Theorem
2.7
, with
A
defined therein,
\rho(A)=A/(n\wedge p\wedge q)^{1/2}
, and

\displaystyle e(A)\equiv 1\bigwedge\bigg(\frac{1\vee A^{2}}{n\wedge p\wedge q}\bigg)^{1/6}\leq(n\wedge p\wedge q)^{-1/6}+1\wedge\rho^{1/3}(A),

we have by using
\Delta_{d}=\mathcal{O}_{M}\big((\tau_{X}\wedge\tau_{Y})^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{n}(t)\leq\inf_{\delta_{1},\delta_{d}\in(0,1/2)}\bigg\{\Phi\bigg[t(1+\delta_{1}+\delta_{d})-\frac{A}{\sqrt{2}}\bigg]+C_{M}\cdot e(A)+\frac{C_{M}^{\prime}(n\wedge p\wedge q)^{-2}}{\delta_{1}^{2}}+\bm{1}_{|\Delta_{d}|\geq\delta_{d}}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq\Phi\bigg(t-\frac{A}{\sqrt{2}}\bigg)+C_{t,M}\bigg[\inf_{\delta_{1}\in(0,1/2]}\Big(\delta_{1}+\frac{(n\wedge p\wedge q)^{-2}}{\delta_{1}^{2}}\Big)+\inf_{\delta_{d}\in(0,1/2]}(\delta_{d}+\bm{1}_{|\Delta_{d}|\geq\delta_{d}})+e(A)\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\Phi\bigg(t-\frac{A}{\sqrt{2}}\bigg)+C_{t,M}\cdot\big[(n\wedge p\wedge q)^{-1/6}+1\wedge\rho^{1/3}(A)\big].

(Estimate 2)
. Note that by Theorem
8.4
and (
12.1
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{n\cdot\tau_{X}\tau_{Y}\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})}{\sqrt{2}\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{A}{\sqrt{2}}\big(1+\mathcal{O}_{M}((p\wedge q)^{-1/2}\big),

\displaystyle\var\bigg[\frac{n\cdot\tau_{X}\tau_{Y}\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})}{\sqrt{2}\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\asymp_{M}1\vee\rho^{2}(A).

Hence by choosing
u=A/(2\sqrt{2}C_{M}(1+\rho(A)))
, and
\delta_{1}=\delta_{d}=1/2
in (
13
), we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{n}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\big(A/\sqrt{2}-C_{M}\cdot u(1+\rho(A))\leq 2t\big)+u^{-2}+C_{M}(n\wedge p\wedge q)^{-2}

\displaystyle\leq\bm{1}_{A\leq 4\sqrt{2}t}+C_{M}\cdot\Big(\big[1+\rho(A)^{2}\big]/A^{2}+(n\wedge p\wedge q)^{-2}\Big).

which implies

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{n}(t)-\Phi\bigg(t-\frac{A}{\sqrt{2}}\bigg)\leq C_{t,M}\bigg(e^{-A^{2}/C}+\frac{1+\rho^{2}(A)}{A^{2}}+\bm{1}_{A\leq 4\sqrt{2}t}+\frac{1}{(n\wedge p\wedge q)^{2}}\bigg).

Combining the estimates (1) and (2), we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{n}(t)-\Phi\bigg(t-\frac{A}{\sqrt{2}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq C_{t,M}\bigg[\bigg((n\wedge p\wedge q)^{-1/6}+1\wedge\rho^{1/3}(A)\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\bigwedge\bigg(e^{-A^{2}/C}+\frac{1}{A^{2}}+\bm{1}_{A\leq 4\sqrt{2}t}+\frac{1}{n\wedge p\wedge q}\bigg)\bigg].

The term in the bracket can be bounded by, via balancing the leading terms
\rho^{1/3}(A)
and
A^{-2}
,

(13.3)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{n}(t)-\Phi\bigg(t-\frac{A}{\sqrt{2}}\bigg)\leq\frac{C_{t,M}}{(n\wedge p\wedge q)^{1/7}}.

By applying similar arguments to the other direction (lower bound) and the case
t\leq 0
, we arrive at

\displaystyle\Big|\mathfrak{p}_{n}(t)-\Phi\bigg(t-\frac{A}{\sqrt{2}}\bigg)\Big|\leq\frac{C_{t,M}}{(n\wedge p\wedge q)^{1/7}},\quad t\in\mathbb{R}.

Finally, using Theorem
8.4
and its analogous expansion to
\dcov^{2}(X),\dcov^{2}(Y)
, we have

\displaystyle n\dcor^{2}(X,Y)=\frac{n\dcov^{2}(X,Y)}{\sqrt{\dcov^{2}(X)\dcov^{2}(Y)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{n\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}^{2}}{\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}}\Big[1+\mathcal{O}_{M}\big((\tau_{X}\wedge\tau_{Y})^{-1}\big)\Big]=A\Big[1+\mathcal{O}_{M}\big((\tau_{X}\wedge\tau_{Y})^{-1}\big)\Big],

the
A
term in (
13.3
) can be replaced by
n\dcor^{2}(X,Y)
at the lost of a larger constant
C_{t,M}
by
[
HJS21
, Lemma 2.4]
.
∎

14.
Proof of Theorem
3.3

Let
\phi_{\Sigma}
denote the Lebesgue density for
\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
. For any prior
\Pi
on
\Theta(\zeta,\Sigma_{0})
, let
P_{\Pi}
the probability measure corresponding to the density
\phi_{\Pi}(x)\equiv\int p_{\Sigma}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\Pi(\Sigma)
. Then

\displaystyle\inf_{\psi}\sup_{\Sigma\in\Theta(\zeta,\Sigma_{0})}\big(\mathbb{E}_{\Sigma_{0}}\psi+\mathbb{E}_{\Sigma}(1-\psi)\big)\geq\inf_{\psi}\big(P_{\Sigma_{0}}(\psi)+P_{\Pi}(1-\psi)\big)

\displaystyle=1-d_{\textrm{TV}}(P_{\Pi},P_{0})\geq 1-\bigg(\int\frac{\phi_{\Pi}^{2}}{\phi_{\Sigma_{0}}}-1\bigg)^{1/2}.

The last inequality follows from, e.g.
[
Tsy09
, Equation (2.27), pp. 90]
.
So the goal is to find the smallest possible
\zeta>0
so that the right hand side of the above display has a non-trivial lower bound under a constructed prior
\Pi
on
\Theta(\zeta,\Sigma_{0})
.

First consider the case
\Sigma_{0}=I
. For any
\widetilde{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
\widetilde{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
, let
u\equiv[\widetilde{u},0_{q}]\in\mathbb{R}^{p+q}
and
v\equiv[0_{p},\widetilde{v}]\in\mathbb{R}^{p+q}
. For any
a>0
, let

\displaystyle\Sigma_{u,v}\equiv\Sigma_{u,v}(a)\equiv I+a\big(uv^{\top}+vu^{\top}\big)=\begin{pmatrix}I&a\widetilde{u}\widetilde{v}^{\top}\\
a\widetilde{v}\widetilde{u}^{\top}&I\end{pmatrix}.

We place independent priors
\widetilde{u}_{i}\sim_{\textrm{i.i.d.}}\sqrt{q}\cdot\textrm{Unif}\{\pm 1\}
on
\widetilde{u}
and
\widetilde{v}_{j}\sim_{\textrm{i.i.d.}}\sqrt{p}\cdot\textrm{Unif}\{\pm 1\}
on
\widetilde{v}
, so that
\lVert\widetilde{u}\rVert^{2}=\lVert u\rVert^{2}=\lVert\widetilde{v}\rVert^{2}=\lVert v\rVert^{2}=pq
and
\left\langle u,v\right\rangle=0
. Then by direct calculation,

\displaystyle(uv^{\top}+vu^{\top})(u+v)=u\cdot\lVert v\rVert^{2}+v\cdot\lVert u\rVert^{2}=pq\cdot(u+v),

\displaystyle(uv^{\top}+vu^{\top})(u-v)=-u\cdot\lVert v\rVert^{2}+v\cdot\lVert u\rVert^{2}=-pq\cdot(u-v).

This entails that the rank two matrix
(uv^{\top}+vu^{\top})
has eigenvalue
pq
with eigenvector
u+v
and eigenvalue
-pq
with eigenvector
u-v
. Hence
\Sigma_{u,v}
has eigenvalues
1+apq
with eigenvector
u+v
,
1-apq
with eigenvector
u-v
, and all rest eigenvalues as
1
, hence
\det(\Sigma_{u,v})=1-(apq)^{2}
. So by writing
\{s_{i}\}_{i=1}^{p+q-2}
as a basis for the orthogonal complement of
u+v
and
u-v
in
\mathbb{R}^{p+q}
, we have the eigenvalue decomposition

\displaystyle\Sigma_{u,v}=\sum_{i=1}^{p+q-2}s_{i}s_{i}^{\top}+(1+apq)\frac{(u+v)(u+v)^{\top}}{\lVert u+v\rVert^{2}}+(1-apq)\frac{(u-v)(u-v)^{\top}}{\lVert u-v\rVert^{2}}.

This implies that

\displaystyle\Sigma_{u,v}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{p+q-2}s_{i}s_{i}^{\top}+\frac{1}{1+apq}\frac{(u+v)(u+v)^{\top}}{\lVert u+v\rVert^{2}}+\frac{1}{1-apq}\frac{(u-v)(u-v)^{\top}}{\lVert u-v\rVert^{2}}

\displaystyle=\Big[I-\frac{(u+v)(u+v)^{\top}}{\lVert u+v\rVert^{2}}-\frac{(u-v)(u-v)^{\top}}{\lVert u-v\rVert^{2}}\Big]

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\frac{1}{1+apq}\frac{(u+v)(u+v)^{\top}}{\lVert u+v\rVert^{2}}+\frac{1}{1-apq}\frac{(u-v)(u-v)^{\top}}{\lVert u-v\rVert^{2}}

\displaystyle=I-\frac{apq}{1+apq}\frac{(u+v)(u+v)^{\top}}{\lVert u+v\rVert^{2}}+\frac{apq}{1-apq}\frac{(u-v)(u-v)^{\top}}{\lVert u-v\rVert^{2}}

\displaystyle=I-\frac{a}{2(1+apq)}(u+v)(u+v)^{\top}+\frac{a}{2(1-apq)}(u-v)(u-v)^{\top}.

Let
\phi_{\Pi}
be the joint density of
\{(X_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
under the prescribed prior on
\widetilde{u}
and
\widetilde{v}
, and
\phi_{I}
be the joint density under the null. With

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Sigma}(u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2})

\displaystyle\equiv-\frac{a}{2(1+apq)}(u_{1}+v_{1})(u_{1}+v_{1})^{\top}+\frac{a}{2(1-apq)}(u_{1}-v_{1})(u_{1}-v_{1})^{\top}

(14.1)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{a}{2(1+apq)}(u_{2}+v_{2})(u_{2}+v_{2})^{\top}+\frac{a}{2(1-apq)}(u_{2}-v_{2})(u_{2}-v_{2})^{\top},

we have

\displaystyle\int\frac{\phi_{\Pi}^{2}}{\phi_{I}}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\Big[\sum_{u,v}2^{-(p+q)}(2\pi)^{-n(p+q)/2}\det^{-n/2}(\Sigma_{u,v})e^{-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{\top}\Sigma_{u,v}^{-1}x_{i}}\Big]^{2}}{(2\pi)^{-n(p+q)/2}\det^{-n/2}(I_{p+q})e^{-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lVert x_{i}\rVert^{2}}}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\sum_{(u_{1},v_{1}),(u_{2},v_{2})}\frac{2^{-2(p+q)}}{\big[(1-(apq)^{2}\big]^{n}}\int(2\pi)^{-n(p+q)/2}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{\top}[\Sigma_{u_{1},v_{1}}^{-1}+\Sigma_{u_{2},v_{2}}^{-1}-I]x_{i}}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{(u_{1},v_{1}),(u_{2},v_{2})}\frac{2^{-2(p+q)}}{\big[1-(apq)^{2}\big]^{n}}\int(2\pi)^{-n(p+q)/2}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{\top}(I+\widetilde{\Sigma}(u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2}))x_{i}}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(*)}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}_{(u_{1},v_{1}),(u_{2},v_{2})}\bigg[\frac{1}{\big[1-(apq)^{2}\big]^{n}}\det\big(I+\widetilde{\Sigma}(u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2})\big)^{-n/2}\bigg].

Here in
(*)
,
(u_{1},v_{1})
and
(u_{2},v_{2})
denote two independent copies of the prescribed prior on
(u,v)
.

By Lemma
14.1
and the notation
\{\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{i}(u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2})\}_{i=1}^{4}
therein,
I+\widetilde{\Sigma}
has
p+q-4
eigenvalues of
1
, and the other four eigenvalues being
1+\lambda_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq 4
. Hence by the second claim of Lemma
14.1
, for any
a
such that
apq\leq 1/4
, we have

\displaystyle\int\frac{\phi_{\Pi}^{2}}{\phi_{I}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{(u_{1},v_{1}),(u_{2},v_{2})}\Big[\frac{1}{\big[1-(apq)^{2}\big]^{n}}\cdot\frac{1}{\big[(1+\lambda_{1})(1+\lambda_{2})(1+\lambda_{3})(1+\lambda_{4})\big]^{n/2}}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{(u_{1},v_{1}),(u_{2},v_{2})}\Big[\frac{1}{\big[1-(apq)^{2}\big]^{n}\cdot\big[1+\frac{a^{2}}{1-(apq)^{2}}(p^{2}q^{2}-\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle)\big]^{n}}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{(u_{1},v_{1}),(u_{2},v_{2})}\Big[\frac{1}{\big(1-a^{2}\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle\big)^{n}}\Big]

\displaystyle=1+n\mathbb{E}\big(a^{2}\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle\big)

\displaystyle\qquad+n(n+1)\mathbb{E}\bigg[\big(a^{2}\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle\big)^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1-t}{\big(1-ta^{2}\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle\big)^{n+2}}\,\mathrm{d}t\bigg].

To calculate the right hand side of the above display, first note that the first order term vanishes:
\mathbb{E}(a^{2}\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle)=0
. Next we compute the second order term. With
\varepsilon_{i}
’s denoting i.i.d. Rademacher random variables,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big(a^{2}\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle\big)^{4}=a^{8}p^{4}q^{4}\cdot\mathbb{E}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{p}\varepsilon_{i}\bigg)^{4}\cdot\mathbb{E}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{q}\varepsilon_{i}\bigg)^{4}\lesssim a^{8}p^{6}q^{6}.

On the other hand, under
a^{2}p^{2}q^{2}\leq 1/2
, there exists some absolute constant
c_{0}>0
such that for any
t\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{1}{\big(1-ta^{2}\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle\big)^{2(n+2)}}\bigg]\leq\mathbb{E}e^{c_{0}a^{2}n\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}e^{c_{0}a^{2}npq\sum_{i=1}^{p}\varepsilon_{i}\sum_{j=1}^{q}\varepsilon_{j}^{\prime}}\leq\mathbb{E}\exp\bigg[(c_{0}^{2}/2)a^{4}n^{2}p^{3}q^{3}\bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}}\sum_{j=1}^{q}\varepsilon_{j}^{\prime}\bigg)^{2}\bigg].

Under
c_{0}^{2}a^{4}n^{2}p^{3}q^{3}\leq 1/2
, the above display is bounded by an absolute constant. Collecting all the estimates, we have established that if

(14.2)

\displaystyle\max\big\{a^{2}p^{2}q^{2},c_{0}^{2}a^{4}n^{2}p^{3}q^{3}\big\}\leq 1/2,

the following bound holds: for some absolute constant
C>0
,

\displaystyle\int\frac{\phi_{\Pi}^{2}}{\phi_{I}}-1\leq C\cdot a^{4}n^{2}p^{3}q^{3}.

Now for any
\delta>0
, by taking
a_{*}=\tau n^{-1/2}p^{-3/4}q^{-3/4}
for some sufficiently small
\tau=\tau(\delta)>0
, we have
\int\phi_{\Pi}^{2}/\phi_{I}-1\leq\delta
and (
14.2
) is satisfied. As
\Sigma_{u,v}(a^{*})\in\Theta(\zeta)
for such choice of
a_{*}
and any realization of
(u,v)
, this completes the proof for the lower bound when
\Sigma_{0}=I
of the order
\lVert a_{*}uv^{\top}\rVert_{F}^{2}=a_{*}^{2}\lVert u\rVert^{2}\lVert v\rVert^{2}=a_{*}^{2}p^{2}q^{2}\asymp\sqrt{pq}/n
. For the general case
\Sigma_{0}
, we may proceed with the above calculations using
\Sigma_{0}^{1/2}\Sigma_{u,v}\Sigma_{0}^{1/2}
instead of
\Sigma_{u,v}
, which gives the same separation rate under the assumption on the spectrum of
\Sigma_{X},\Sigma_{Y}
. ∎

Lemma 14.1
.

The matrix
\widetilde{\Sigma}(u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2})
defined in (
14
) has at most 4 nontrivial eigenvalues of the form:

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}

\displaystyle=(c_{2}-c_{1})(pq+\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle)-\beta_{i}(c_{1}+c_{2})(\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle+pq),\quad i=1,2,

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}

\displaystyle=(c_{2}-c_{1})(pq-\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle)+\beta_{i}(c_{1}+c_{2})(\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle-pq),\quad i=3,4.

Here
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
and
(\beta_{3},\beta_{4})
are solutions to equations (
14
) and (
14
) below, respectively. Furthermore,

\displaystyle(1+\lambda_{1})(1+\lambda_{2})=(1+\lambda_{3})(1+\lambda_{4})=1+\frac{a^{2}}{1-(apq)^{2}}(p^{2}q^{2}-\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle).

Proof of Lemma
14.1
.

We write
\widetilde{\Sigma}(u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2})
as
\widetilde{\Sigma}
in the proof for simplicity. Define

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle\equiv\alpha_{1}u_{1}+\alpha_{2}u_{2}+\alpha_{3}v_{1}+\alpha_{4}v_{2},\quad c_{1}\equiv\frac{a}{2(1+apq)},\quad c_{2}=\frac{a}{2(1-apq)}.

Then

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Sigma}

\displaystyle\equiv-c_{1}\Big[(u_{1}+v_{1})(u_{1}+v_{1})^{\top}+(u_{2}+v_{2})(u_{2}+v_{2})^{\top}\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+c_{2}\Big[(u_{1}-v_{1})(u_{1}-v_{1})^{\top}+(u_{2}-v_{2})(u_{2}-v_{2})^{\top}\Big],

and

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Sigma}b\equiv T_{1}u_{1}+T_{2}u_{2}+T_{3}u_{3}+T_{4}u_{4},

where

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{1}\cdot(c_{2}-c_{1})pq+\alpha_{2}\cdot(c_{2}-c_{1})\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle-\alpha_{3}\cdot(c_{1}+c_{2})pq-\alpha_{4}\cdot(c_{1}+c_{2})\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{1}\cdot(c_{2}-c_{1})\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle+\alpha_{2}\cdot(c_{2}-c_{1})pq-\alpha_{3}\cdot(c_{1}+c_{2})\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle-\alpha_{4}\cdot(c_{1}+c_{2})pq,

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle=-\alpha_{1}\cdot(c_{1}+c_{2})pq-\alpha_{2}\cdot(c_{1}+c_{2})\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle+\alpha_{3}\cdot(c_{2}-c_{1})pq+\alpha_{4}\cdot(c_{2}-c_{1})\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle T_{4}

\displaystyle=-\alpha_{1}\cdot(c_{1}+c_{2})\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle-\alpha_{2}\cdot(c_{1}+c_{2})pq+\alpha_{3}\cdot(c_{2}-c_{1})\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle+\alpha_{4}\cdot(c_{2}-c_{1})pq.

Hence the identity
T_{1}/\alpha_{1}=T_{2}/\alpha_{2}=T_{3}/\alpha_{3}=T_{4}/\alpha_{4}
is equivalent to (denoting
\rho_{ij}\equiv\alpha_{i}/\alpha_{j}
)

(i)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\rho_{21}\cdot(c_{2}-c_{1})\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle-\rho_{31}\cdot(c_{1}+c_{2})pq-\rho_{41}\cdot(c_{1}+c_{2})\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle

(ii)

\displaystyle=\rho_{12}\cdot(c_{2}-c_{1})\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle-\rho_{32}\cdot(c_{1}+c_{2})\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle-\rho_{42}\cdot(c_{1}+c_{2})pq

(iii)

\displaystyle=-\rho_{13}\cdot(c_{1}+c_{2})pq-\rho_{23}\cdot(c_{1}+c_{2})\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle+\rho_{43}\cdot(c_{2}-c_{1})\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle

(iv)

\displaystyle=-\rho_{14}\cdot(c_{1}+c_{2})\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle-\rho_{24}\cdot(c_{1}+c_{2})pq+\rho_{34}\cdot(c_{2}-c_{1})\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle.

For the first set of two solutions, let
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=1
and
\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{4}=\beta
. Then
(i)=(ii)
and
(iii)=(iv)
automatically holds, and
(ii)=(iii)
is equivalent to

\displaystyle(c_{1}+c_{2})(\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle+pq)\cdot\beta^{2}+(c_{2}-c_{1})(\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle-\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle)\cdot\beta

(14.3)

\displaystyle\quad\quad-(c_{1}+c_{2})(\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle+pq)=0.

Note that as
|\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle|\leq\lVert v_{1}\rVert\lVert v_{2}\rVert=pq
and similarly for
|\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle|
, we have

\displaystyle(c_{2}-c_{1})^{2}(\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle-\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle)^{2}+4(c_{1}+c_{2})^{2}(\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle+pq)(\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle+pq)\geq 0.

Hence (
14
) has two (possible identical) solutions, and these two solutions denoted by
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
lead to two eigenvectors of
\widetilde{\Sigma}
. For the second set of two solutions, let
\alpha_{1}=-\alpha_{2}=1
and
\alpha_{3}=-\alpha_{4}=\beta
. Then again
(i)=(ii)
and
(iii)=(iv)
automatically, and
(ii)=(iii)
is equivalent to

\displaystyle(c_{1}+c_{2})(\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle-pq)\cdot\beta^{2}+(c_{2}-c_{1})(\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle-\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle)\cdot\beta

(14.4)

\displaystyle\quad\quad-(c_{1}+c_{2})(\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle-pq)=0.

The two (possible identical) solutions of the above quadratic equation (existence due to similar reason as (
14
)), denoted by
\beta_{3}
and
\beta_{4}
, lead to the other two eigenvectors. Summarizing the above arguments,
\widetilde{\Sigma}
has four eigenvalue

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}

\displaystyle=(c_{2}-c_{1})(pq+\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle)-\beta_{i}(c_{1}+c_{2})(\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle+pq),\quad i=1,2,

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}

\displaystyle=(c_{2}-c_{1})(pq-\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle)+\beta_{i}(c_{1}+c_{2})(\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle-pq),\quad i=3,4.

For the second claim of the lemma, note that (
14
) implies that

\displaystyle\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{(c_{2}-c_{1})(\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle-\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle)}{(c_{1}+c_{2})(\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle+pq)},

\displaystyle\beta_{1}\cdot\beta_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{(c_{1}+c_{2})(\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle+pq)}{(c_{1}+c_{2})(\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle+pq)}.

Hence we have

\displaystyle(1+\lambda_{1})(1+\lambda_{2})

\displaystyle=\Big(\big[1+(c_{2}-c_{1})(pq+\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle)\big]-\beta_{1}(c_{1}+c_{2})(\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle+pq)\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\cdot\Big(\big[1+(c_{2}-c_{1})(pq+\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle)\big]-\beta_{2}(c_{1}+c_{2})(\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle+pq)\Big)

\displaystyle=\big[1+(c_{2}-c_{1})(pq+\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle)\big]^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad-(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2})(c_{1}+c_{2})(\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle+pq)\big[1+(c_{2}-c_{1})(pq+\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle)\big]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\cdot(c_{1}+c_{2})(\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle+pq)^{2}

\displaystyle=\big[1+(c_{2}-c_{1})(pq+\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle)\big]^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad-(c_{2}-c_{1})(\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle-\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle)\big[1+(c_{2}-c_{1})(pq+\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle)\big]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad-(c_{1}+c_{2})^{2}(\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle+pq)(\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle+pq)

\displaystyle=1+(c_{2}-c_{1})(2pq+\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle+\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle)-4c_{1}c_{2}(\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle+pq)(\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle+pq).

By plugging in the definition of
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle(1+\lambda_{1})(1+\lambda_{2})

\displaystyle=1+\frac{a^{2}pq}{1-(apq)^{2}}(2pq+\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle+\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad-\frac{a^{2}}{1-(apq)^{2}}(\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle+pq)(\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle+pq)

\displaystyle=1+\frac{a^{2}}{1-(apq)^{2}}(p^{2}q^{2}-\left\langle u_{1},u_{2}\right\rangle\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle).

The argument for the expression
(1+\lambda_{3})(1+\lambda_{4})
is similar, so the proof is complete.
∎

Appendix A
Proof of Proposition
2.1

Lemma A.1
.

Recall
A=\{A_{ij}=\lVert X_{i}-X_{j}\rVert\}
and
B=\{B_{ij}=\lVert Y_{i}-Y_{j}\rVert\}
and
A^{\ast},B^{\ast}
defined in (
2.1
). Then

\displaystyle\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})=\frac{1}{n(n-3)}\sum_{k\neq\ell}A_{k\ell}^{\ast}B_{k\ell}^{\ast}=\frac{1}{n(n-3)}\bigg(\tr(AB)+\frac{\bm{1}^{\top}A\bm{1}\bm{1}^{\top}B\bm{1}}{(n-1)(n-2)}-\frac{2\bm{1}^{\top}AB\bm{1}}{n-2}\bigg).

This result is known in the literature, see e.g. in the proof of
[
YZS18
, Lemma 2.1]
. We provide a complete proof for the convenience of the reader.

Proof.

Recall
A\equiv\{A_{ij}\}\equiv\{\lVert X_{i}-X_{j}\rVert\}
and
B\equiv\{B_{ij}\|\equiv\lVert Y_{i}-Y_{j}\rVert
. Note that
\sum_{k\neq\ell}A_{k\ell}^{*}B_{k\ell}^{*}=\tr(A^{*}B^{*})-\sum_{k=1}^{n}(A^{*})_{kk}(B^{*})_{kk}
. The second term can be computed as follows: As

\displaystyle A_{kk}^{*}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{n-2}e_{k}^{\top}A\bm{1}+\frac{1}{(n-1)(n-2)}\bm{1}^{\top}A\bm{1},

we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}A_{kk}^{*}B_{kk}^{*}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\Big(-\frac{2}{n-2}e_{k}^{\top}A\bm{1}+\frac{1}{(n-1)(n-2)}\bm{1}^{\top}A\bm{1}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\Big(-\frac{2}{n-2}e_{k}^{\top}B\bm{1}+\frac{1}{(n-1)(n-2)}\bm{1}^{\top}B\bm{1}\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{(n-2)^{2}}\bm{1}^{\top}AB\bm{1}+\Big(-\frac{4}{(n-1)(n-2)^{2}}+\frac{n}{(n-1)^{2}(n-2)^{2}}\Big)\cdot\bm{1}^{\top}A\bm{1}\bm{1}^{\top}B\bm{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{(n-2)^{2}}\bm{1}^{\top}AB\bm{1}-\frac{3n-4}{(n-1)^{2}(n-2)^{2}}\bm{1}^{\top}A\bm{1}\bm{1}^{\top}B\bm{1}.

For the first term, using the definition of
A^{\ast},B^{\ast}
,

\displaystyle\tr(A^{*}B^{*})

\displaystyle=\tr\Big[\Big(A-\frac{1}{n-2}\bm{1}\bm{1}^{\top}A-\frac{1}{n-2}A\bm{1}\bm{1}^{\top}+\frac{\bm{1}^{\top}A\bm{1}}{(n-1)(n-2)}\bm{1}\bm{1}^{\top}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\Big(B-\frac{1}{n-2}\bm{1}\bm{1}^{\top}B-\frac{1}{n-2}B\bm{1}\bm{1}^{\top}+\frac{\bm{1}^{\top}B\bm{1}}{(n-1)(n-2)}\bm{1}\bm{1}^{\top}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\tr(AB)+\frac{8-2n}{(n-2)^{2}}\bm{1}^{\top}AB\bm{1}+\frac{n^{2}-6n+6}{(n-1)^{2}(n-2)^{2}}\bm{1}^{\top}A\bm{1}\bm{1}^{\top}B\bm{1}.

Combining the two identities above to conclude.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.1
.

By the proceeding lemma,

\displaystyle\dcov_{\ast}^{2}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})=\frac{1}{n(n-3)}\bigg(\tr(AB)+\frac{\bm{1}^{\top}A\bm{1}\bm{1}^{\top}B\bm{1}}{(n-1)(n-2)}-\frac{2\bm{1}^{\top}AB\bm{1}}{n-2}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n(n-3)}\bigg(\sum_{i\neq j}A_{ij}B_{ij}+\frac{1}{(n-1)(n-2)}\sum_{i\neq j,k\neq\ell}A_{ij}B_{k\ell}-\frac{2}{n-2}\sum_{i\neq j,i\neq k}A_{ij}B_{ik}\bigg)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(*)}}{{=}}\frac{1}{n(n-3)}\bigg(\frac{n-3}{n-1}\sum_{i\neq j}A_{ij}B_{ij}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{(n-1)(n-2)}\sum_{i\neq j\neq k\neq\ell}A_{ij}B_{k\ell}-\frac{2(n-3)}{(n-1)(n-2)}\sum_{i\neq j\neq k}A_{ij}B_{ik}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4!\binom{n}{4}}\sum_{i\neq j\neq k\neq\ell}\big(A_{ij}B_{ij}+A_{ij}B_{k\ell}-2A_{ij}B_{ik}\big)=\frac{1}{\binom{n}{4}}\sum_{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{4}}k_{0}\big(Z_{i_{1}},Z_{i_{2}},Z_{i_{3}},Z_{i_{4}}\big),

Here
(*)
follows from the identities:

\displaystyle\sum_{i\neq j,i\neq k}A_{ij}B_{ik}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\neq j\neq k}A_{ij}B_{ik}+\sum_{i\neq j}A_{ij}B_{ij},

\displaystyle\sum_{i\neq j,k\neq\ell}A_{ij}B_{k\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\neq j\neq k\neq\ell}A_{ij}B_{k\ell}+4\sum_{i\neq j\neq k}A_{ij}B_{ik}+2\sum_{i\neq j}A_{ij}B_{ij},

and
k_{0}
is the symmetrized kernel

\displaystyle k_{0}(Z_{1},Z_{2},Z_{3},Z_{4})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4!}\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{4})\in\sigma(1,2,3,4)}\lVert X_{i_{1}}-X_{i_{2}}\rVert\big(\lVert Y_{i_{1}}-Y_{i_{2}}\rVert+\lVert Y_{i_{3}}-Y_{i_{4}}\rVert-2\lVert Y_{i_{1}}-Y_{i_{3}}\rVert\big)

\displaystyle\equiv\mathbb{E}_{\bm{i}\in\sigma_{4}}(i_{1},i_{2})_{X}\big[(i_{1},i_{2})_{Y}+(i_{3},i_{4})_{Y}-2(i_{1},i_{3})_{Y}\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{i}\in\sigma_{4}}(i_{1},i_{2})_{X}\big[(i_{1},i_{2})_{Y}+(i_{3},i_{4})_{Y}-2(i_{2},i_{4})_{Y}\big]\quad(1\leftrightarrow 2,3\leftrightarrow 4),

where we abbreviate
(i,j)_{X}=(X_{i},X_{j}),(i,j)_{Y}=(Y_{i},Y_{j})
and the expectation
\mathbb{E}_{\bm{i}\in\sigma_{4}}
is taken over uniform distribution of permutation group over
\{1,2,3,4\}
. So by averaging the above two equations, we have

\displaystyle k_{0}(\bm{Z})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{i}\in\sigma_{4}}(i_{1},i_{2})_{X}\big[(i_{1},i_{2})_{Y}+(i_{3},i_{4})_{Y}-(i_{1},i_{3})_{Y}-(i_{2},i_{4})_{Y}\big]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{i}\in\sigma_{4}}(i_{3},i_{4})_{X}\big[(i_{1},i_{2})_{Y}+(i_{3},i_{4})_{Y}-(i_{1},i_{3})_{Y}-(i_{2},i_{4})_{Y}\big]\quad(a:1\leftrightarrow 3,2\leftrightarrow 4)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{i}\in\sigma_{4}}(i_{1},i_{3})_{X}\big[(i_{1},i_{3})_{Y}+(i_{2},i_{4})_{Y}-(i_{1},i_{2})_{Y}-(i_{3},i_{4})_{Y}\big]\quad(a:2\leftrightarrow 3)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{i}\in\sigma_{4}}(i_{2},i_{4})_{X}\big[(i_{1},i_{3})_{Y}+(i_{2},i_{4})_{Y}-(i_{1},i_{2})_{Y}-(i_{3},i_{4})_{Y}\big]\quad(c:1\leftrightarrow 3,2\leftrightarrow 4).

Using that

\displaystyle(i_{1},i_{2})_{X}+(i_{3},i_{4})_{X}-(i_{1},i_{3})_{X}-(i_{2},i_{4})_{X}

\displaystyle=U(X_{i_{1}},X_{i_{2}})+U(X_{i_{3}},X_{i_{4}})-U(X_{i_{1}},X_{i_{3}})-U(X_{i_{2}},X_{i_{4}}),

and a similar equation for
(\cdot,\cdot)_{Y}
replacing
U
by
V
, we have

\displaystyle k_{0}(\bm{Z})=\frac{1}{4\cdot 4!}\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{4})\in\sigma(1,2,3,4)}\Big(U(X_{i_{1}},X_{i_{2}})+U(X_{i_{3}},X_{i_{4}})-U(X_{i_{1}},X_{i_{3}})-U(X_{i_{2}},X_{i_{4}})\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\Big(V(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{i_{2}})+V(Y_{i_{3}},Y_{i_{4}})-V(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{i_{3}})-V(Y_{i_{2}},Y_{i_{4}})\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\cdot 4!}\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{4})\in\sigma(1,2,3,4)}\bigg[U(X_{i_{1}},X_{i_{2}})\Big(V(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{i_{2}})+V(Y_{i_{3}},Y_{i_{4}})-V(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{i_{3}})-V(Y_{i_{2}},Y_{i_{4}})\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad+U(X_{i_{3}},X_{i_{4}})\Big(V(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{i_{2}})+V(Y_{i_{3}},Y_{i_{4}})-V(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{i_{3}})-V(Y_{i_{2}},Y_{i_{4}})\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad-U(X_{i_{1}},X_{i_{3}})\Big(V(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{i_{2}})+V(Y_{i_{3}},Y_{i_{4}})-V(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{i_{3}})-V(Y_{i_{2}},Y_{i_{4}})\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad-U(X_{i_{2}},X_{i_{4}})\Big(V(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{i_{2}})+V(Y_{i_{3}},Y_{i_{4}})-V(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{i_{3}})-V(Y_{i_{2}},Y_{i_{4}})\Big)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4!}\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{4})\in\sigma(1,2,3,4)}\bigg[U(X_{i_{1}},X_{i_{2}})V(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{i_{2}})+U(X_{i_{1}},X_{i_{2}})V(Y_{i_{3}},Y_{i_{4}})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-2U(X_{i_{1}},X_{i_{2}})V(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{i_{3}})\bigg]=k(\bm{Z}).

The proof is complete.
∎

Appendix B
Proofs for Section
7

B.1.
Proof of Lemma
7.2

Proof of Lemma
7.2
.

We first prove (1). Note that under the spectral condition
\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{2}\lVert\Sigma_{Y}\rVert_{F}^{2}\asymp_{M}pq
, so it suffices to show that all three terms on the left side is bounded by the order of
p\vee q
. For the first term, we have

\displaystyle\tr(G_{[12]}\circ G_{[12]})=\sum_{i=1}^{p+q}(\Sigma^{1/2}e_{1}\Sigma_{XY}e_{2}^{\top}\Sigma^{1/2})_{ii}^{2}\leq(p+q)\lVert\Sigma^{1/2}e_{1}\Sigma_{XY}e_{2}^{\top}\Sigma^{1/2}\rVert_{\op}^{2}\lesssim_{M}p\vee q.

The second term can be controlled similarly. For the third term, using
\max_{i,j}|(H_{[11]})_{i,j}|\vee|(H_{[22]})_{i,j}|\lesssim_{M}1
,

\displaystyle\lVert H_{[11]}\circ H_{[22]}\rVert_{F}^{2}\lesssim_{M}\lVert H_{[11]}\rVert_{F}^{2}\wedge\lVert H_{[22]}\rVert_{F}^{2}\lesssim_{M}p\wedge q.

Next we prove (2). Let
\Delta\equiv\Sigma^{1/2}-\Sigma_{0}^{1/2}
, where
\Sigma_{0}\equiv[\Sigma_{X},0;0,\Sigma_{Y}]
. Then the proof of Lemma
8.3
shows that
\lVert\Delta\rVert_{F}\lesssim_{M}\lVert\Sigma_{XY}\rVert_{F}
. Expanding the definitions of
G_{[11]}
and
G_{[22]}
, we have

\displaystyle\lVert G_{[11]}\circ G_{[22]}\rVert_{F}

\displaystyle=\lVert(\Sigma_{0}^{1/2}+\Delta)\Sigma_{[11]}(\Sigma_{0}^{1/2}+\Delta)\circ(\Sigma_{0}^{1/2}+\Delta)\Sigma_{[22]}(\Sigma_{0}^{1/2}+\Delta)\rVert_{F}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{D_{1},\ldots,D_{4}\in\{\Sigma_{0}^{1/2},\Delta\}}\lVert D_{1}\Sigma_{[11]}D_{2}\circ D_{3}\Sigma_{[22]}D_{4}\rVert_{F}.

The claim now follows from the inequality
\lVert A\circ B\rVert_{F}\leq\lVert A\rVert_{\op}\lVert B\rVert_{F}
for (not necessarily symmetric) matrices
A,B
, and by noting that the summand is zero if at most one of
D_{1}
-
D_{4}
is
\Delta
.
∎

B.2.
Proof of Lemma
7.3

The bound for
h
follows by considering two regimes
u\in[-1,-1/2]
and
u>-1/2
separately. In particular, for
u\in[-1,-1/2]
,
\lvert h(u)\rvert=\lvert\sqrt{1+u}-1-u/2\rvert\lesssim 1
. For
u>-1/2
,
\inf_{s\in[0,1]}(1+su)^{3/2}\gtrsim 1
so using the second equality of (
7.1
) leads to the bound
\lvert h(u)\rvert\lesssim u^{2}
. Combining the two cases to conclude
\lvert h(u)\rvert\lesssim u^{2}
for
u>-1
. Next, as

\displaystyle h^{\prime\prime}(u)=-\frac{1}{4(1+u)^{3/2}},\,h^{(3)}(u)=\frac{3}{8(1+u)^{5/2}},\,h^{(4)}(u)=-\frac{15}{16(1+u)^{7/2}},

by Taylor expansion,

\displaystyle h(u)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\int_{0}^{u}\frac{u-t}{(1+t)^{3/2}}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{h^{\prime\prime}(0)}{2}u^{2}+u^{3}\int_{0}^{1}h^{(3)}(su)\frac{(1-s)^{2}}{2}\,\mathrm{d}s=-\frac{u^{2}}{8}+u^{3}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{3(1-s)^{2}}{16(1+su)^{5/2}}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\frac{h^{\prime\prime}(0)}{2}u^{2}+\frac{h^{(3)}(0)}{6}u^{3}+u^{4}\int_{0}^{1}h^{(4)}(su)\frac{(1-s)^{3}}{6}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=-\frac{u^{2}}{8}+\frac{u^{3}}{16}-u^{4}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{5(1-s)^{3}}{32(1+su)^{7/2}}\,\mathrm{d}s.

The bound for
h^{\prime}
follows by noting that
h^{\prime}(u)=2^{-1}(1-\sqrt{1+u})/\sqrt{1+u}
and
|1-\sqrt{1+u}|\lesssim|u|
.∎

B.3.
Proof of Lemma
7.4

The first two equations follow from the definition. For the second two equations, note that by definition of
U(x_{1},x_{2})
,

\displaystyle U(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\tau_{X}}{2}\bigg[\lVert x_{1}-x_{2}\rVert^{2}/\tau_{X}^{2}-\mathbb{E}\big[\lVert x_{1}-X\rVert^{2}/\tau_{X}^{2}\big]

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}\big[\lVert X-x_{2}\rVert^{2}/\tau_{X}^{2}\big]+\mathbb{E}\big[\lVert X-X^{\prime}\rVert^{2}/\tau_{X}^{2}\big]+2\bar{R}_{X}(x_{1},x_{2})\bigg]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\tau_{X}}\Big(x_{1}^{\top}x_{2}-\tau_{X}^{2}\bar{R}_{X}(x_{1},x_{2})\Big),

as desired. Similar derivation applies to
V(y_{1},y_{2})
.∎

B.4.
Proof of Lemma
7.5

(1). By Jensen’s inequality, it suffices to consider the case where
s=2^{\ell}
for some
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, which we will prove by induction. For the baseline case
\ell=1
, it follows by Lemma
G.3
-(1) that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}L_{X}^{2}

\displaystyle=\tau_{X}^{-4}\mathbb{E}\big(\lVert X_{1}-X_{2}\rVert^{2}-\tau_{X}^{2}\big)^{2}=4\tau_{X}^{-4}\cdot\Big(\mathbb{E}\big[\frac{1}{2}(Z_{X,1}-Z_{X,2})^{\top}\Sigma_{X}(Z_{X,1}-Z_{X,2})\big]^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\tau_{X}^{4}\Big)

\displaystyle=4\tau_{X}^{-4}\cdot(2\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{2}+(\widetilde{m}_{4}-3)\tr(\Sigma_{X}\circ\Sigma_{X}))\lesssim_{m_{4}}\tau_{X}^{-4}\lVert\Sigma_{X}\rVert_{F}^{2}.

Here
\widetilde{m}_{4}\equiv\mathbb{E}\big[\big((Z_{1,X})_{1}-(Z_{2,X})_{1}\big)/\sqrt{2}\big]^{4}\lesssim m_{4}
. For general
\ell
, using the Poincaré inequality of
Z
, we have

\displaystyle\tau_{X}^{2^{\ell+1}}\mathbb{E}L_{X}^{2^{\ell}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\big(\lVert X_{1}-X_{2}\rVert^{2}-\tau_{X}^{2}\big)^{2^{\ell}}\lesssim_{\ell}\mathbb{E}\big(Z_{X}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}Z_{X}-\tr(\Sigma_{X})\big)^{2^{\ell}}

\displaystyle=\Big(\mathbb{E}\big(Z_{X}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}Z_{X}-\tr(\Sigma_{X})\big)^{2^{\ell-1}}\Big)^{2}+\var\Big(\big(Z_{X}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}Z_{X}-\tr(\Sigma_{X})\big)^{2^{\ell-1}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\mathbb{E}\big(Z_{X}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}Z_{X}-\tr(\Sigma_{X})\big)^{2^{\ell-1}}\Big)^{2}+c_{*}\mathbb{E}\Big[2^{\ell-1}\big(Z_{X}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}Z_{X}-\tr(\Sigma_{X})\big)^{2^{\ell-1}-1}\cdot 2\lVert\Sigma_{X}Z_{X}\rVert\Big]^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\mathbb{E}\big(Z_{X}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}Z_{X}-\tr(\Sigma_{X})\big)^{2^{\ell-1}}\Big)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(Z_{X}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}Z_{X}-\tr(\Sigma_{X})\big)^{2^{\ell}}\Big]+K(\ell,c_{*})\mathbb{E}\lVert\Sigma_{X}Z_{X}\rVert^{2^{\ell}}.

Here
c_{*}
is the Poincaré constant of
Z
. Rearranging the terms yields that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(Z_{X}^{\top}\Sigma_{X}Z_{X}-\tr(\Sigma_{X})\big)^{2^{\ell}}\Big]
